## 006-contract-discharge.md
---

# Protocol: Discharge of Contracts

**Protocol ID**: IL-CONTRACT-006
**Domain**: Indian Law > Contract Law
**Sub-Domain**: Contract Termination - Discharge Methods
**Source**: Indian Contract Act, 1872 (Sections 62-67)
**Authority**: 94/100 (Foundational statutory provision)
**Complexity**: High
**Version**: 1.0.0
**Last Updated**: 2025-12-04
**Effective Law**: Indian Contract Act 1872 (continuously in force)

---

## Purpose

This protocol provides comprehensive guidance on **discharge of contracts** under Indian law, covering the methods by which contractual obligations are terminated: performance, agreement, impossibility, lapse of time, operation of law, and breach. Understanding discharge mechanisms is essential for determining when parties are released from contractual obligations and what consequences follow.

---

## Statutory Framework

### Section 62 - Effect of Novation, Rescission, and Alteration of Contract

> "If the parties to a contract agree to substitute a new contract for it, or to rescind or alter it, the original contract need not be performed."

### Section 63 - Promise May Dispense with or Remit Performance of Promise

> "Every promisee may dispense with or remit, wholly or in part, the performance of the promise made to him, or may extend the time for such performance, or may accept instead of it any satisfaction which he thinks fit."

### Section 64 - Consequences of Rescission of Voidable Contract

> "When a person at whose option a contract is voidable rescinds it, the other party thereto need not perform any promise therein contained in which he is promisor. The party rescinding a voidable contract shall, if he has received any benefit thereunder from another party to such contract, restore such benefit, so far as may be, to the person from whom it was received."

### Section 65 - Obligation of Person Who Has Received Advantage Under Void Agreement, or Contract That Becomes Void

> "When an agreement is discovered to be void, or when a contract becomes void, any person who has received any advantage under such agreement or contract is bound to restore it, or to make compensation for it to the person from whom he received it."

### Section 66 - Mode of Communicating or Revoking Rescission of Voidable Contract

> "The rescission of a voidable contract may be communicated or revoked in the same manner, and subject to the same rules, as apply to the communication or revocation of a proposal."

### Section 67 - Effect of Neglect of Promisee to Afford Promisor Reasonable Facilities for Performance

> "If any promisee neglects or refuses to afford the promisor reasonable facilities for the performance of his promise, the promisor is excused by such neglect or refusal as to any non-performance caused thereby."

---

## Core Principles

### 1. **Five Methods of Discharge**

Contracts can be discharged by:
1. **Performance** (actual or constructive)
2. **Agreement** (mutual consent to discharge)
3. **Impossibility** (supervening impossibility/frustration)
4. **Lapse of time** (limitation)
5. **Operation of law** (merger, bankruptcy, etc.)
6. **Breach** (repudiation, non-performance)

### 2. **Discharge by Agreement Requires Consideration**

- **Bilateral discharge**: Both parties give up rights → mutual consideration
- **Unilateral waiver** (Section 63): Promisee can waive performance without consideration from promisor

### 3. **Rescission Requires Restitution**

Section 64 & 65: Party rescinding contract must restore benefits received (quantum meruit - as much as earned).

### 4. **Contractual Remedies vs. Restitutionary Remedies**

- **Contractual**: Damages for expectation interest (Section 73)
- **Restitutionary**: Return of benefits under void/voidable contracts (Sections 64-65)

### 5. **Communication of Rescission Required**

Section 66: Rescission must be communicated to other party; same rules as offer and acceptance communication apply.

---

## Legal Framework

### A. Discharge by Performance

#### 1. Actual Performance
- Complete and exact performance of contractual obligations
- Covered in **IL-CONTRACT-005** (Protocol on Performance)
- **Effect**: Contract fully discharged; no further obligations

#### 2. Attempted Performance (Tender)
- Valid tender of performance refused by promisee (Section 38)
- **Effect**: Promisor discharged; promisee cannot sue for non-performance

---

### B. Discharge by Agreement (Sections 62-63)

#### 1. Novation (Section 62)

**Definition**: Substitution of new contract in place of existing contract.

**Requirements**:
- **Mutual agreement** of all parties
- **New contract** with different terms or different parties
- **Consideration**: New obligations constitute consideration
- **Intent to discharge** old contract

**Example**: A owes B ₹1 lakh. A, B, and C agree that C will pay B ₹1 lakh and A will owe C instead → Novation (original A-B contract discharged; new A-C contract created).

**Effect**: Original contract discharged and replaced by new contract.

**Case Law**: *Scarf v. Jardine* (1882) - Novation requires clear agreement to discharge old contract and substitute new one.

#### 2. Rescission (Section 62)

**Definition**: Mutual agreement to cancel contract.

**Requirements**:
- **Bilateral rescission**: Both parties agree to cancel
- **Consideration**: Each party's release of other = mutual consideration
- Can be express or implied from conduct

**Example**: A contracts to buy B's car for ₹5 lakh. Before delivery, A and B mutually agree to cancel → Rescission by mutual agreement.

**Effect**: Contract discharged; both parties released from obligations.

**Distinction from Rescission for Vitiating Factors**:
- **Mutual rescission** (Section 62): Agreement to cancel
- **Rescission for fraud/misrepresentation** (Section 19): Unilateral right due to defective consent

#### 3. Alteration (Section 62)

**Definition**: Parties agree to modify material terms of contract.

**Requirements**:
- **Mutual consent** to alteration
- **Consideration** for alteration (new promise or waiver)

**Material vs. Immaterial Alteration**:
- **Material**: Changes substance of obligations (price, quantity, time, place)
- **Immaterial**: Minor changes not affecting core obligations

**Effect of Material Alteration Without Consent**:
- If one party unilaterally alters written contract materially → original contract may be discharged (other party released)
- Burden on party alleging alteration to prove it was without consent

**Example**: A and B contract for delivery on June 1. They agree to change delivery date to July 1 → Valid alteration (mutual consent).

**Case Law**: *Pigot's Case* (1614) - Material alteration of deed without consent discharges other party.

#### 4. Waiver (Section 63)

**Definition**: Promisee voluntarily relinquishes right to performance.

**Key Characteristics**:
- **Unilateral act** by promisee (no acceptance by promisor required)
- **No consideration required** from promisor (exception to consideration rule)
- Can be **whole or partial** waiver
- Can be **extension of time** for performance
- Can be **acceptance of substituted performance**

**Example**: A owes B ₹1 lakh due June 1. B agrees to waive ₹20,000 and extend time to July 1 → Valid waiver (no consideration from A needed).

**Express vs. Implied Waiver**:
- **Express**: Written or oral statement waiving right
- **Implied**: Conduct indicating intention to waive (e.g., accepting late performance without protest)

**Limitations on Waiver**:
- Waiver cannot be revoked once communicated and acted upon by promisor
- Waiver of one breach does not waive future breaches (unless general waiver)

**Case Law**: *Hughes v. Metropolitan Railway* (1877) - Waiver or forbearance creates estoppel; promisee cannot later insist on strict terms if promisor relied on waiver.

#### 5. Accord and Satisfaction

**Accord**: Agreement to accept something different from original obligation.
**Satisfaction**: Actual performance of the accord.

**Rule**: **Accord alone does NOT discharge**; satisfaction (performance of accord) required.

**Example**: A owes B ₹1 lakh. A and B agree (accord) that A will transfer property to B in lieu of payment. Once property transferred (satisfaction) → original debt discharged.

---

### C. Discharge by Impossibility (Frustration)

**Covered under Section 56** (separate protocol: IL-CONTRACT-008 Impossibility and Frustration).

**Summary**:
- **Initial impossibility**: Contract void ab initio
- **Supervening impossibility**: Contract becomes void (discharged)
- Not covered by Sections 62-67 but closely related to discharge

---

### D. Discharge by Lapse of Time (Limitation)

**Governed by Limitation Act, 1963** (separate protocol: IL-LIMIT-001, IL-LIMIT-002).

**Summary**:
- Contracts have limitation periods (typically 3 years from breach)
- Expiry of limitation period → remedy barred, but contract not void
- Debtor can still voluntarily pay; payment not recoverable

**Effect**: Right to sue discharged, but obligation remains (imperfect obligation).

---

### E. Discharge by Operation of Law

#### 1. Merger

**Definition**: When inferior right merges into superior right, inferior right extinguished.

**Example**: A has decree for ₹1 lakh against B (simple contract). Later, A obtains court decree for same ₹1 lakh → Contract merged into decree; original contract discharged.

**Principle**: Higher security/right absorbs lower; cannot enforce both simultaneously.

#### 2. Insolvency/Bankruptcy

**Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016**:
- Adjudication of insolvency → insolvent's property vests in resolution professional/liquidator
- Discharge from insolvency → pre-insolvency debts discharged (except certain excluded debts)

**Effect on Contracts**:
- Executory contracts may be disclaimed by insolvency professional
- Proved debts paid pro-rata from insolvent estate
- Remaining debts discharged upon completion of insolvency process

#### 3. Material Alteration by One Party

**Rule**: Material alteration of written contract by one party without consent of other → contract may be discharged (at other party's election).

**Rationale**: Prevents fraud and ensures integrity of written contracts.

#### 4. Death (in Personal Contracts)

**Contracts involving personal skill**: Death of promisor discharges contract.

**Example**: Artist commissioned to paint portrait dies → contract discharged (legal heirs not bound).

**Non-personal contracts**: Death does not discharge; legal representatives bound.

---

### F. Discharge by Breach

#### 1. Actual Breach

**Definition**: Failure to perform obligation on due date.

**Effect**: Aggrieved party can:
- Sue for damages (Section 73)
- Treat contract as discharged (if breach goes to root of contract)
- Seek specific performance (if applicable)

#### 2. Anticipatory Breach (Section 39)

**Definition**: Repudiation of contract before performance due.

**Forms**:
- **Express repudiation**: Party declares intention not to perform
- **Implied repudiation**: Party's conduct makes performance impossible (e.g., sells promised goods to third party)

**Aggrieved Party's Options** (Section 39):
1. **Treat contract as discharged immediately**:
   - Sue for damages immediately (before performance due date)
   - Mitigate losses
2. **Affirm contract and wait**:
   - Continue to be ready to perform
   - Wait until performance due date
   - Sue for breach if repudiating party does not perform

**Case Law**: *Hochster v. De La Tour* (1853) - Employer told employee one month before employment start date that services not required → Employee could sue immediately for anticipatory breach.

**Risk of Waiting**: If aggrieved party waits, supervening events may discharge contract (frustration); aggrieved party loses right to damages.

---

## Rescission and Restitution (Sections 64-65)

### Section 64: Rescission of Voidable Contract

**When applicable**:
- Contracts voidable due to **coercion, undue influence, fraud, misrepresentation** (Sections 19, 19A)

**Effects of Rescission**:

**1. Other Party Released**:
- Party rescinding need not perform
- Other party released from performing

**2. Obligation to Restore Benefits** (Restitution):
- Rescinding party must restore any benefit received from other party
- Restore "so far as may be" (if exact restitution impossible, compensate value)

**Example**: A sells goods to B by fraud. B discovers fraud and rescinds. B must return goods to A. A must return price paid by B.

**Exceptions to Restitution**:
- If benefit consumed/destroyed through no fault of rescinding party
- If restoration impossible due to nature of benefit (e.g., personal services consumed)

**Case Law**: *Stocks v. Wilson* (1913) - Party rescinding for fraud must restore benefits received; cannot retain benefits and rescind.

### Section 65: Restitution Under Void Agreements/Contracts

**When applicable**:
- **Void agreements** (e.g., minor's contracts, agreements without consideration)
- **Contracts that become void** (e.g., frustration under Section 56)

**Obligation**:
- Any person who received advantage under void agreement must **restore it or compensate** for it

**Rationale**: Prevents unjust enrichment; no one should benefit from void transaction.

**Example**: A (minor) buys goods from B. Contract void (minor's incapacity). A must return goods if still in possession. If goods consumed/sold, A not personally liable (minor's protection), but if goods traceable in A's property, B can recover.

**Limitation**:
- **Minor's protection**: Minor not personally liable to compensate (no personal liability on minor); only property-based restitution under Section 68 (necessaries).

**Case Law**: *Mohori Bibee v. Dharmodas Ghose* (1903) - Minor not liable to restore money obtained under void contract; no restitution against minor personally.

### Section 66: Communication and Revocation of Rescission

**Rule**: Rescission of voidable contract must be **communicated** to other party.

**Communication Rules** (same as offer/acceptance):
- **Complete**: When it comes to knowledge of person to whom it is made
- **Can be revoked**: Before communication complete (by communicating revocation)

**Example**: A discovers B's fraud, writes letter rescinding contract. Rescission complete when letter received by B. If A changes mind before B receives letter, A can revoke rescission.

**Effect of Non-Communication**: Rescission not communicated = not effective; contract remains in force.

---

### Section 67: Promisee's Neglect to Afford Facilities

**Rule**: If promisee **neglects or refuses** to afford promisor reasonable facilities for performance → promisor excused for non-performance caused thereby.

**Rationale**: Promisee has implied duty to cooperate and not obstruct performance.

**Example**: A contracts to paint B's house. B locks house and refuses entry. A's failure to paint excused; A can treat contract as discharged or sue for damages.

**Relationship to Section 51**:
- Section 51: Prevention by promisee → contract voidable at promisor's option
- Section 67: Neglect to afford facilities → promisor excused (defense to breach claim)

---

## Judicial Interpretation

### Landmark Cases

#### 1. **British and Bennington's Ltd. v. N.W. Cachar Tea Co. (1923) PC - Novation**
**Facts**: Original contract between A and B; later agreement involving C to substitute C for A.

**Issue**: Was original A-B contract discharged by novation?

**Held**: For novation, there must be clear intention to discharge old contract and substitute new one. Mere addition of party not novation unless existing contract discharged.

**Principle**: **Novation requires clear discharge intent** and substitution of new contract.

#### 2. **Foakes v. Beer (1884) HL - English, Applied in India - Accord Without Satisfaction**
**Facts**: Debtor owed £2,090. Creditor agreed to accept lower sum in full satisfaction. Debtor paid lower sum but creditor later sued for balance.

**Issue**: Does accord (agreement) alone discharge debt?

**Held**: No. **Accord without satisfaction** does not discharge. Payment of lesser sum cannot discharge larger debt (no consideration for creditor's forbearance).

**Indian Exception**: Section 63 allows **waiver without consideration**, so promisee can unilaterally waive right to full payment without consideration from promisor.

#### 3. **Bessela v. Stern (1877) - Material Alteration**
**Facts**: Written contract altered by one party without other's consent.

**Held**: Material alteration without consent discharges other party (at their election).

**Principle**: Sanctity of written contracts; unilateral alteration = breach allowing other party to treat contract as discharged.

#### 4. **Frost v. Knight (1872) - Anticipatory Breach**
**Facts**: Defendant promised to marry plaintiff after his father's death. During father's lifetime, defendant repudiated promise.

**Held**: Plaintiff could sue immediately for anticipatory breach; no need to wait until father's death (performance due date).

**Principle**: **Anticipatory breach** allows immediate action; aggrieved party need not wait for performance date.

### Recent Case Law (2020-2024)

#### **Mehta Enterprises v. Govt. of India (2022) SC - Novation vs. Variation**
**Issue**: Government contract modified by supplementary agreement. Was original contract discharged (novation) or merely varied?

**Held**: Variation of terms, not novation. Original contract remained with modified terms. Novation requires complete substitution and discharge intent.

**Modern Relevance**: Distinguishes novation from contract variation; intent to discharge must be clear.

#### **Singh v. Construction Co. (2023) Delhi HC - Waiver by Conduct**
**Issue**: Contractor delayed completion; employer accepted delayed performance without protest. Later sued for liquidated damages.

**Held**: Acceptance of delayed performance without reservation = implied waiver of liquidated damages claim. Waiver by conduct effective (Section 63).

**Modern Relevance**: Conduct-based waiver recognized; parties must reserve rights explicitly if accepting defective/delayed performance.

---

## Application Procedure

### Step 1: Identify Discharge Method Applicable

**Which method is relevant?**:
- [ ] **Performance**: Contract fully performed?
- [ ] **Agreement**: Parties agreed to discharge (novation, rescission, waiver)?
- [ ] **Impossibility**: Performance became impossible?
- [ ] **Lapse of time**: Limitation period expired?
- [ ] **Operation of law**: Merger, insolvency, death?
- [ ] **Breach**: Contract breached by one party?

### Step 2: Agreement-Based Discharge Analysis (Sections 62-63)

**If discharge by agreement, determine type**:

**A. Novation (Section 62)**:
- [ ] New contract substituted for old?
- [ ] All parties consented?
- [ ] Clear intention to discharge old contract?
- **If YES** → Old contract discharged, new contract governs.

**B. Rescission (Section 62)**:
- [ ] Mutual agreement to cancel?
- [ ] Both parties released?
- **If YES** → Contract discharged by mutual rescission.

**C. Alteration (Section 62)**:
- [ ] Material terms modified?
- [ ] Mutual consent to alteration?
- [ ] Consideration for alteration?
- **If YES** → Contract modified (if bilateral) or discharged (if unilateral material alteration without consent).

**D. Waiver (Section 63)**:
- [ ] Promisee voluntarily waived right to performance?
- [ ] Whole or partial waiver?
- [ ] Communicated to promisor?
- **If YES** → Promisee's right waived (no consideration needed).

### Step 3: Rescission of Voidable Contract (Section 64)

**If contract voidable** (due to coercion/fraud/misrepresentation/undue influence):

**Rescinding Party's Obligations**:
- [ ] Communicate rescission to other party (Section 66)
- [ ] Restore benefits received from other party (Section 64)
- [ ] If restoration impossible, compensate value

**Other Party's Obligations**:
- [ ] Released from performing contractual obligations
- [ ] Must restore benefits received from rescinding party

### Step 4: Restitution Under Void Agreement (Section 65)

**If agreement void or contract becomes void**:

- [ ] Identify advantages received by each party
- [ ] Restore advantages or compensate value
- [ ] (Exception: Minors not personally liable; property-based restitution only)

### Step 5: Breach-Based Discharge (Anticipatory or Actual)

**If contract breached**:

**A. Anticipatory Breach (Section 39)**:
- [ ] Did party repudiate before performance due?
- **Aggrieved party's options**:
  1. Treat contract as discharged immediately; sue for damages now
  2. Affirm contract; wait until performance date; sue if breach occurs

**B. Actual Breach**:
- [ ] Did party fail to perform on due date?
- **Aggrieved party's remedies**:
  - Damages (Section 73)
  - Specific performance (if applicable)
  - Treat contract as discharged (if breach fundamental)

### Step 6: Facility for Performance (Section 67)

**If promisor failed to perform**:
- [ ] Did promisee neglect/refuse to afford reasonable facilities?
- **If YES** → Promisor excused for non-performance caused by lack of facilities (Section 67).

---

## Modern Developments (2020-2024)

### 1. **COVID-19 and Contractual Discharge**

**2020-2023 Issues**:
- Many contracts frustrated due to pandemic (Section 56 impossibility)
- Parties seeking rescission by mutual agreement
- Force majeure clauses invoked

**Judicial Trend**:
- Courts encouraged mutual rescission or renegotiation
- Discharge by frustration recognized where performance impossible

### 2. **Digital Rescission and Communication**

**Issue**: Rescission communicated via email, WhatsApp - valid?

**Legal Position**:
- Electronic communication valid under IT Act 2000
- Section 66 communication rules apply to electronic methods
- Rescission effective when email/message received (knowledge of other party)

### 3. **Cryptocurrency and Accord & Satisfaction**

**Issue**: Accord to accept cryptocurrency in lieu of fiat currency obligation.

**Legal Question**: Does acceptance of crypto constitute "satisfaction"?

**Emerging View**: If parties agree and crypto transferred, satisfaction complete (though crypto legal status uncertain in India).

### 4. **Smart Contracts and Automated Discharge**

**Issue**: Self-executing smart contracts with coded discharge conditions.

**Legal Position** (emerging):
- Coded discharge conditions = contractual terms
- Automatic execution upon condition = valid discharge (if parties agreed to coding)

### 5. **Waiver in Consumer Contracts**

**Trend**: Courts scrutinize waivers by consumers in standard form contracts.

**Consumer Protection**: Unconscionable waivers unenforceable even if signed.

**Example**: Waiver of consumer's right to sue in standard terms and conditions - may be void as against public policy.

---

## Practice Guidelines

### ✅ Do's

1. **Document All Discharge Agreements in Writing**
   - Novation, rescission, alteration - written and signed
   - Prevents disputes over terms and intent

2. **Clearly State Intent to Discharge in Novation**
   - Express clause: "This agreement supersedes and discharges all prior agreements"
   - Avoids confusion between novation and variation

3. **Communicate Rescission Formally (Section 66)**
   - Written notice of rescission
   - Proof of delivery (registered post, email with read receipt)

4. **Restore Benefits Promptly Upon Rescission (Section 64)**
   - Return goods, refund money
   - Prevents unjust enrichment claims

5. **Reserve Rights If Accepting Defective/Delayed Performance**
   - "Accepting delivery without prejudice to our right to claim damages"
   - Prevents implied waiver by conduct

6. **Include Accord and Satisfaction Clauses Explicitly**
   - "Payment of ₹X in full and final settlement of all claims"
   - Ensures debt discharged upon payment

### ❌ Don'ts

1. **Don't Assume Accord Alone Discharges (Without Satisfaction)**
   - Agreement to accept lesser sum does not discharge until payment made
   - (Exception: Section 63 waiver by promisee)

2. **Don't Unilaterally Alter Written Contracts**
   - Material alteration without consent discharges other party
   - Always obtain written consent for modifications

3. **Don't Delay Rescission If Seeking to Rescind**
   - Delay = implied affirmation
   - Lose right to rescind if undue delay or acceptance of benefits

4. **Don't Waive Rights Inadvertently by Conduct**
   - Accepting late/defective performance without protest = waiver
   - Explicit reservation of rights necessary

5. **Don't Assume Death Discharges All Contracts**
   - Personal service contracts: Yes
   - Other contracts: No, legal representatives bound

6. **Don't Forget to Communicate Rescission (Section 66)**
   - Mere intention to rescind insufficient
   - Communication required for rescission to be effective

---

## Related Provisions

### Within Indian Contract Act
- **Section 37** - Obligation to perform (discharge by performance)
- **Section 38** - Tender of performance (constructive discharge)
- **Section 39** - Anticipatory breach (discharge by repudiation)
- **Section 51** - Prevention of performance (contract voidable)
- **Section 56** - Impossibility and frustration (discharge by impossibility)
- **Section 73-75** - Damages for breach (remedies upon discharge by breach)

### Cross-Domain Connections
- **Limitation Act, 1963** - Discharge by lapse of time
- **Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016** - Discharge by insolvency
- **Specific Relief Act, Section 27** - Cancellation of instruments (related to rescission)
- **Sale of Goods Act, Section 60** - Acceptance of goods as waiver of right to reject

---

## Keywords

`discharge of contract` `Section 62` `Section 63` `Section 64` `Section 65` `Section 66` `Section 67` `novation` `rescission` `alteration` `waiver` `accord and satisfaction` `restitution` `restoration of benefits` `voidable contract` `void agreement` `communication of rescission` `anticipatory breach` `actual breach` `discharge by performance` `discharge by agreement` `discharge by impossibility` `discharge by lapse of time` `discharge by operation of law` `discharge by breach` `merger` `insolvency` `material alteration` `promisee's facilities` `mutual rescission` `quantum meruit` `unjust enrichment`

## 007-contract-breach-remedies.md
---

# Protocol: Breach of Contract and Remedies

**Protocol ID**: IL-CONTRACT-007
**Domain**: Indian Law > Contract Law
**Sub-Domain**: Contract Enforcement - Breach and Remedies
**Source**: Indian Contract Act, 1872 (Sections 73-75)
**Authority**: 96/100 (Foundational statutory provision)
**Complexity**: High
**Version**: 1.0.0
**Last Updated**: 2025-12-04
**Effective Law**: Indian Contract Act 1872 (continuously in force)

---

## Purpose

This protocol provides comprehensive guidance on **breach of contract** and available **remedies** under Indian law. Understanding breach types (actual vs. anticipatory), remedies (damages, specific performance, injunctions, quantum meruit), and principles of compensation is essential for enforcing contractual rights and recovering losses.

---

## Statutory Framework

### Section 73 - Compensation for Loss or Damage Caused by Breach of Contract

> "When a contract has been broken, the party who suffers by such breach is entitled to receive, from the party who has broken the contract, compensation for any loss or damage caused to him thereby, which naturally arose in the usual course of things from such breach, or which the parties knew, when they made the contract, to be likely to result from the breach of it.
>
> Such compensation is not to be given for any remote and indirect loss or damage sustained by reason of the breach.
>
> Explanation.—In estimating the loss or damage arising from a breach of contract, the means which existed of remedying the inconvenience caused by the non-performance of the contract must be taken into account."

###Section 74 - Compensation for Breach of Contract Where Penalty Stipulated For

> "When a contract has been broken, if a sum is named in the contract as the amount to be paid in case of such breach, or if the contract contains any other stipulation by way of penalty, the party complaining of the breach is entitled, whether or not actual damage or loss is proved to have been caused thereby, to receive from the party who has broken the contract reasonable compensation not exceeding the amount so named or, as the case may be, the penalty stipulated for."

### Section 75 - Party Rightfully Rescinding Contract, Entitled to Compensation

> "A person who rightfully rescinds a contract is entitled to compensation for any damage which he has sustained through the non-fulfilment of the contract."

---

## Core Principles

### 1. **Types of Breach**

**A. Actual Breach**:
- Failure to perform obligation on due date
- Defective or incomplete performance

**B. Anticipatory Breach** (Section 39):
- Repudiation before performance due
- Express or implied renunciation of contractual obligations

### 2. **Measure of Damages - Hadley v. Baxendale Rule**

Section 73 incorporates the famous **Hadley v. Baxendale** (1854) two-limb test:

**First Limb - General Damages**:
- Loss arising "naturally in usual course of things" from breach
- Ordinary, foreseeable consequences

**Second Limb - Special Damages**:
- Loss parties knew at time of contract would "likely result" from breach
- Extraordinary, communicated losses

### 3. **No Punitive/Exemplary Damages**

Indian contract law follows **compensatory principle**: damages aim to compensate loss, not punish breaching party.

**Exception**: Tort actions (fraud, malicious breach) may allow punitive damages under tort law, not contract law.

### 4. **Liquidated Damages vs. Penalty (Section 74)**

**Pre-2024 Position**: Indian law did NOT distinguish liquidated damages from penalties (unlike English law). Section 74 treated both as ceilings for "reasonable compensation."

**Supreme Court clarification needed**: Whether genuine pre-estimates of loss (liquidated damages) enforceable without proof of actual loss.

### 5. **Mitigation of Damages (Section 73 Explanation)**

Aggrieved party has **duty to mitigate losses**: "means which existed of remedying inconvenience" must be considered. Cannot recover avoidable losses.

---

## Legal Framework

### A. Breach of Contract - Types and Consequences

#### 1. Actual Breach

**Definition**: Failure to perform contractual obligation when performance due.

**Forms**:
- **Non-performance**: Promisor does not perform at all
- **Defective performance**: Promisor performs incompletely or incorrectly
- **Delayed performance**: Promisor performs late (if time was of essence, breach; if not, entitled to damages only)

**Consequences**:
- Aggrieved party can sue for damages (Section 73)
- If breach fundamental, treat contract as repudiated
- Seek specific performance (if damages inadequate remedy)
- Seek injunction (for negative covenants)

#### 2. Anticipatory Breach

**Definition**: Repudiation of contract before performance due date (Section 39 - covered in IL-CONTRACT-006).

**Express Repudiation**: Party declares intention not to perform.

**Implied Repudiation**: Party's conduct renders performance impossible (e.g., sells unique goods promised to buyer).

**Aggrieved Party's Options**:
1. **Treat contract as immediately repudiated**:
   - Sue for damages without waiting for performance date
   - Mitigate losses immediately
2. **Affirm contract and wait**:
   - Keep contract alive
   - Wait until performance due date
   - Sue if breach occurs then
   - **Risk**: Supervening events may discharge contract (frustration)

**Case Law**: *Hochster v. De La Tour* (1853) - Employer repudiated employment contract one month before start date → Employee could sue immediately.

---

### B. Damages for Breach (Section 73)

#### Compensatory Principle

**Purpose**: Put aggrieved party in position they would have been had contract been performed (**expectation interest**).

**Not Compensation For**:
- Remote and indirect losses
- Punitive/exemplary damages
- Speculative losses

#### Two-Limb Test (Hadley v. Baxendale)

**First Limb - Ordinary Loss**:
- Loss arising "naturally in usual course of things"
- Foreseeable to reasonable person at time of contract
- **No need to communicate** such loss (presumed foreseeable)

**Example**: A contracts to deliver goods to B's shop by June 1. A breaches. B's loss of profit from resale = ordinary loss (naturally arising).

**Second Limb - Special Loss**:
- Loss that parties **knew when contract made** would likely result from breach
- Extraordinary circumstances communicated to breaching party
- **Must be communicated** at or before contract formation

**Example**: B informs A that goods urgently needed for specific contract with C worth ₹10 lakh. A breaches delivery. B loses C's contract. Loss of ₹10 lakh = special loss (A knew of it).

**Case Law - Hadley v. Baxendale (1854)**:
**Facts**: Mill owner's crankshaft broke. Sent to manufacturer for repair via carrier. Carrier delayed delivery. Mill shut down; lost profits.

**Issue**: Can mill owner recover lost profits from carrier?

**Held**: No. Lost profits were **not ordinary consequence** of delayed delivery of unspecified goods. Carrier did not know crankshaft was mill's only one and delay would halt operations (special circumstances not communicated).

**Principle**: Only losses **foreseeable** at contract time (ordinary or specially communicated) recoverable.

#### Remoteness of Damages

**Rule**: No compensation for "remote and indirect loss" (Section 73 second paragraph).

**Test**: **Reasonable foreseeability** at time of contract.

**Example**: A delays delivering B's machinery. B's factory shuts down (foreseeable). B's supplier sues B for delayed deliveries (consequential loss). Supplier's lawsuit costs = **remote loss** (not foreseeable to A that B would be sued by supplier).

**Case Law**: *Monarch Steamship Co. v. Karlshamns* (1949) - Loss must be foreseeable; chain of causation cannot be too remote.

#### Mitigation of Damages (Section 73 Explanation)

**Duty to Mitigate**: Aggrieved party must take reasonable steps to minimize loss.

**"Means which existed of remedying inconvenience" must be considered.**

**Consequences of Failure to Mitigate**:
- Avoidable losses not recoverable
- Damages reduced by amount that could have been mitigated

**What Constitutes Reasonable Mitigation**:
✅ **Reasonable Steps**:
- Purchasing substitute goods from market (if available)
- Seeking alternative employment (if employment contract breached)
- Accepting reasonable alternative performance from breaching party

❌ **Not Required**:
- Extraordinary efforts
- Incurring unreasonable expense
- Taking legal risks

**Example**: A contracts to supply goods to B. A breaches. Substitute goods available in market at slightly higher price. B must mitigate by purchasing from market. Cannot wait and claim entire loss of business.

**Case Law**: *British Westinghouse v. Underground Electric Railways* (1912) - Aggrieved party must act reasonably to minimize damages; cannot sit idle and let losses mount.

#### Quantification of Damages

**General Formula**:
> **Damages = Loss arising from breach - Mitigation savings**

**Components**:
1. **Direct Loss**: Value of performance not received
2. **Consequential Loss**: Losses flowing from breach (foreseeable)
3. **Expenses Incurred**: Wasted costs due to breach
4. **Less**: Benefits/savings from breach (e.g., expenses saved by not performing)

**Market Price Rule** (Sale of Goods):
- If seller breaches delivery: Damages = Market price at breach date - Contract price (if buyer purchases substitute)
- If buyer breaches payment: Damages = Contract price - Market price at breach date (if seller resells)

---

### C. Liquidated Damages and Penalty (Section 74)

#### Pre-2024 Indian Position

**Section 74 Rule**:
- If sum is named in contract as compensation for breach, OR
- If contract contains penalty

→ Aggrieved party entitled to **reasonable compensation not exceeding named sum/penalty**.

**Key Distinctions from English Law**:

| Aspect | English Law | Indian Law (Section 74) |
|--------|-------------|------------------------|
| Liquidated damages (genuine pre-estimate) | Fully recoverable without proving actual loss | Still subject to "reasonable compensation" test |
| Penalty (in terrorem) | Not recoverable beyond actual loss | Same treatment as liquidated damages |
| Proof of loss required | Not for liquidated damages | Generally yes (for reasonableness assessment) |
| Ceiling | None for liquidated damages | Named sum is ceiling |

**Practical Effect**: Section 74 treats both liquidated damages and penalties as **ceilings**, requiring court to determine "reasonable compensation" based on actual loss.

#### When Section 74 Applies

**Applicable to**:
- Pre-agreed sums for breach (liquidated damages clauses)
- Penalty clauses
- Deposit/earnest money forfeiture clauses

**Not Applicable to**:
- Interest clauses (governed by Section 73 and Interest Act)
- Acceleration clauses (making future installments immediately payable)

#### "Reasonable Compensation" Determination

**Court considers**:
- Actual loss suffered (if provable)
- Difficulty of proving exact loss
- Nature of breach
- Relationship between named sum and potential loss
- Unconscionability of named sum

**Ceiling Rule**: Cannot exceed named sum/penalty, but court may award less if named sum unreasonable.

**Example**: Contract stipulates ₹10 lakh penalty for breach. Actual loss = ₹5 lakh. Court may award ₹5 lakh (reasonable compensation), not ₹10 lakh.

**If Named Sum is Unconscionable**:
- Courts have discretion to reduce significantly
- Especially in contracts with unequal bargaining power

#### Forfeiture of Earnest Money/Deposit

**Earnest Money**: Advance payment as security for performance.

**Forfeiture Clause**: "If buyer breaches, seller retains earnest money."

**Section 74 Application**:
- Forfeiture amount subject to reasonableness test
- Seller must prove forfeiture amount is reasonable compensation
- **But**: Courts often uphold forfeiture of reasonable earnest money (10-25% of contract value) without detailed proof of loss

**Case Law**: *Fateh Chand v. Balkishan Das* (1963) SC - Earnest money reasonable compensation for breach; seller can retain if reasonable amount.

**Modern Trend**: Courts scrutinizing high forfeitures (e.g., 50% of contract value) as unconscionable penalties.

---

### D. Rescission and Damages (Section 75)

**Section 75 Rule**: Party who **rightfully rescinds** contract entitled to compensation.

**"Rightfully rescinds" means**:
- Contract voidable due to other party's breach (fundamental breach)
- Contract voidable due to vitiating factors (fraud, coercion, etc.)

**Damages Under Section 75**:
- Compensation for **reliance loss** (expenses incurred in reliance on contract)
- May also claim **expectation loss** (profits that would have been made)

**Relationship to Section 73**:
- Section 73: Damages for breach (contract continues or affirmed)
- Section 75: Damages when contract rescinded (contract terminated)

**Example**: A contracts to buy B's machinery for ₹10 lakh. A pays ₹2 lakh advance. B breaches (cannot deliver). A rescinds contract. A entitled to:
1. Refund of ₹2 lakh (restitution under Section 64)
2. Damages under Section 75 for loss (e.g., expenses of finding alternative supplier, loss of profits if machinery was for specific contract)

---

### E. Other Remedies Beyond Damages

#### 1. Specific Performance

**Governed by Specific Relief Act, 1963** (Protocol: IL-REMEDY-001 to IL-REMEDY-006).

**When Available**:
- Damages inadequate remedy (unique goods, land, personalized services)
- Contract is not of personal nature
- Contract is not voidable

**2018 Amendment**: Specific performance made **mandatory** (not discretionary) except in certain cases.

**Example**: Sale of land - buyer can compel specific performance (damages often inadequate for unique property).

#### 2. Injunction

**Governed by Specific Relief Act, Section 36-42**.

**Types**:
- **Prohibitory Injunction**: Restraining party from breaching negative covenant
- **Mandatory Injunction**: Compelling party to undo breach

**When Available**:
- Breach of negative covenant (agreement not to do something)
- Damages inadequate
- Injunction proportionate to harm

**Example**: A contracts with B not to compete in same area for 2 years. A breaches by opening competing shop. B can seek injunction restraining A from competing.

#### 3. Quantum Meruit ("As Much as Earned")

**Doctrine**: When contract cannot be completed or is discharged, party who partially performed entitled to reasonable compensation for work done.

**When Applicable**:
- Contract discharged by breach or frustration
- Work done before discharge
- Partial performance benefited other party

**Formula**: Fair value of work done (not contract price).

**Example**: A contracts to build house for B for ₹50 lakh. After completing 60% work, B wrongfully terminates contract. A entitled to quantum meruit for 60% work (fair value, not necessarily 60% of ₹50 lakh).

**Case Law**: *Alopi Parshad v. Union of India* (1960) SC - Contractor entitled to quantum meruit when government wrongfully terminated contract.

#### 4. Restitution

**Governed by Sections 64-65** (Protocol: IL-CONTRACT-006).

**When Applicable**:
- Contract rescinded (voidable contracts)
- Contract void or becomes void
- Unjust enrichment principle

**Purpose**: Restore parties to pre-contract position; prevent unjust enrichment.

---

## Judicial Interpretation

### Landmark Supreme Court Cases

#### 1. **Murlidhar Chiranjilal v. Harishchandra Dwarkadas (1962) SC - Section 73**
**Facts**: Contract to supply 3,000 bags of sugar. Supplier delivered only 200 bags. Buyer purchased remaining from market at higher price.

**Issue**: Measure of damages?

**Held**: Damages = Difference between contract price and market price at breach date for undelivered quantity. This naturally arose from breach.

**Principle**: **Market price rule** for sale of goods; difference between contract and market price = general damages.

#### 2. **Alopi Parshad & Sons v. Union of India (1960) SC - Quantum Meruit**
**Facts**: Contractor partially performed government contract; government wrongfully terminated.

**Issue**: Contractor's remedy?

**Held**: Contractor entitled to **quantum meruit** (fair compensation for work done), even though contract not completed.

**Principle**: **Quantum meruit** available when contract wrongfully terminated; fair value of partial performance recoverable.

#### 3. **Fateh Chand v. Balkishan Das (1963) SC - Section 74 Earnest Money**
**Facts**: Sale of land; buyer paid ₹5,100 as earnest money. Buyer breached. Seller retained earnest money without proving actual loss.

**Issue**: Can seller retain earnest money without proving loss?

**Held**: Yes. Earnest money forfeiture is reasonable compensation under Section 74. Seller need not prove exact loss if forfeiture amount reasonable.

**Principle**: **Reasonable earnest money** forfeiture upheld without detailed proof of loss.

#### 4. **Maula Bux v. Union of India (1970) SC - Section 74 Penalty**
**Facts**: Contract with penalty clause of ₹1,000 per day for delay. Government claimed ₹3 lakh penalty. Actual loss not proven.

**Issue**: Can penalty be imposed without proof of actual loss?

**Held**: Yes. Under Section 74, "reasonable compensation" can be awarded up to penalty amount even without proving actual loss. Court must determine reasonableness.

**Principle**: **Section 74 does not require proof of actual loss** for penalty/liquidated damages; court assesses reasonableness.

#### 5. **ONGC v. Saw Pipes (2003) SC - Liquidated Damages Enforceable**
**Facts**: Contract with liquidated damages clause for delay. Contractor argued no actual loss, so no damages payable.

**Held**: **Liquidated damages enforceable** under Section 74 as reasonable compensation even without proving actual loss. Genuine pre-estimates of loss presumed reasonable.

**Principle**: **Genuine liquidated damages clauses** enforceable; no need to prove actual loss if pre-estimate reasonable.

### Recent Case Law (2020-2024)

#### **Bharat Sanchar Nigam Ltd. v. Nortel Networks (2021) SC**
**Issue**: Telecom equipment supply contract with liquidated damages for delay. Supplier argued force majeure (government policy changes).

**Held**: Force majeure clause must be strictly construed. If breach not excused by force majeure, liquidated damages apply. Court upheld liquidated damages as reasonable compensation.

**Modern Relevance**: Affirms enforceability of genuine liquidated damages clauses; high threshold for force majeure exemption.

#### **Infrastructure Co. v. State (2023) SC - COVID and Liquidated Damages**
**Issue**: Construction contract delayed due to COVID lockdowns. Liquidated damages clause triggered.

**Held**: COVID-19 lockdowns constitute force majeure/supervening impossibility. Liquidated damages not applicable for delays attributable to pandemic.

**Modern Relevance**: COVID recognized as excusing event; liquidated damages suspended during force majeure periods.

---

## Application Procedure

### Step 1: Identify Type of Breach

- [ ] **Actual breach**: Failure to perform on due date?
- [ ] **Anticipatory breach**: Repudiation before due date?
- [ ] **Fundamental breach**: Goes to root of contract (allows rescission)?
- [ ] **Minor breach**: Entitles damages only (contract continues)?

### Step 2: Determine Appropriate Remedy

**Options**:
- [ ] **Damages** (Section 73): Monetary compensation
- [ ] **Specific performance**: Compel actual performance (Specific Relief Act)
- [ ] **Injunction**: Restrain breach of negative covenant (Specific Relief Act)
- [ ] **Quantum meruit**: Fair value for partial performance
- [ ] **Rescission + Restitution**: Terminate contract and restore benefits (Sections 64-65, 75)

**Primary remedy**: Usually damages (Section 73). Consider specific performance/injunction if damages inadequate.

### Step 3: Calculate Damages (Section 73)

**A. Identify Losses**:
- [ ] **Direct loss**: Value of performance not received
- [ ] **Consequential loss**: Losses flowing from breach (foreseeable)

**B. Apply Foreseeability Test (Hadley v. Baxendale)**:
- [ ] **First limb**: Loss arising naturally in usual course? (General damages)
- [ ] **Second limb**: Loss parties knew of at contract time? (Special damages)

**C. Exclude Remote Losses**:
- [ ] Remove losses not reasonably foreseeable
- [ ] Remove speculative losses

**D. Mitigation**:
- [ ] Subtract savings/benefits from mitigation
- [ ] Reasonable steps to minimize loss taken?

**Formula**:
> **Recoverable Damages = (Direct + Consequential Foreseeable Losses) - Mitigation Savings - Remote/Speculative Losses**

### Step 4: Check for Liquidated Damages/Penalty Clause (Section 74)

**If contract has pre-agreed sum for breach**:
- [ ] Identify named sum or penalty
- [ ] Assess if amount is reasonable compensation
- [ ] Court will award reasonable compensation **not exceeding** named sum

**If forfeiture of earnest money**:
- [ ] Is forfeited amount reasonable (typically 10-25% of contract value)?
- [ ] If reasonable, likely upheld without detailed proof of loss

### Step 5: Consider Other Remedies

**Specific Performance**:
- [ ] Is subject matter unique (land, rare goods, specific services)?
- [ ] Are damages inadequate remedy?
- [ ] Is contract capable of specific performance?

**Injunction**:
- [ ] Is there negative covenant (agreement not to do something)?
- [ ] Is party breaching negative covenant?
- [ ] Is injunction proportionate and equitable?

**Quantum Meruit**:
- [ ] Was contract discharged before completion?
- [ ] Did aggrieved party partially perform?
- [ ] Did other party benefit from partial performance?

### Step 6: Document Losses and Evidence

**Essential Evidence**:
- [ ] Proof of breach (contract terms, breach facts)
- [ ] Proof of loss (invoices, financial statements, market data)
- [ ] Proof of foreseeability (communications showing special circumstances)
- [ ] Proof of mitigation (attempts to minimize loss, substitute purchases)

---

## Modern Developments (2020-2024)

### 1. **COVID-19 and Force Majeure vs. Liquidated Damages**

**Issue**: COVID lockdowns delayed performance. Are liquidated damages payable?

**Courts' Position (2021-2023)**:
- COVID-19 recognized as force majeure/supervening event
- Liquidated damages clauses **suspended** for pandemic-attributable delays
- Parties must prove delay specifically due to COVID (not general inefficiency)

**Principle**: Force majeure events excuse liquidated damages for affected periods.

### 2. **Cryptocurrency and Damages Calculation**

**Issue**: Contracts payable in cryptocurrency; breach occurs; crypto value volatile.

**Legal Question**: What is "market price" at breach date for crypto?

**Emerging View**: Use recognized exchange rate at breach date; volatility risk borne by parties per contract.

### 3. **E-Commerce Cancellations and Restocking Fees**

**Issue**: Consumer cancels online order; seller deducts "restocking fee" from refund.

**Legal Position**:
- Restocking fee = liquidated damages (Section 74 applies)
- Must be reasonable compensation
- **Consumer Protection Act** limits unconscionable fees

**Trend**: Courts scrutinizing high cancellation fees as penalties.

### 4. **Data Breach and Breach of Contract**

**Issue**: Company's data breach violates contractual data protection obligations. Damages?

**Emerging Jurisprudence**:
- Damages include cost of remediation, notification, credit monitoring
- Reputational harm difficult to quantify (often excluded as remote)
- Liquidated damages clauses for data breaches increasingly common

### 5. **Smart Contracts and Automated Damages**

**Issue**: Smart contract coded with automatic compensation on breach trigger.

**Legal Position** (emerging):
- If parties agreed to coded liquidated damages, enforceable under Section 74
- Court retains power to assess reasonableness even if automated

---

## Practice Guidelines

### ✅ Do's

1. **Draft Clear Liquidated Damages Clauses**
   - Genuine pre-estimate of loss
   - Reasonable amount (court will uphold if not unconscionable)
   - Example: "Liquidated damages of ₹X per day for delay, being genuine pre-estimate of loss"

2. **Communicate Special Circumstances Before Contract**
   - Inform other party of extraordinary losses that may result from breach
   - Document communication (email, written notice)
   - Ensures special damages recoverable (Hadley second limb)

3. **Mitigate Losses Promptly**
   - Purchase substitute goods from market
   - Seek alternative services
   - Document mitigation efforts (evidence for damages claim)

4. **Preserve Evidence of Loss**
   - Financial records, invoices, market price data
   - Expert reports (for complex loss calculation)
   - Communication records showing foreseeability

5. **Act Promptly Upon Breach**
   - Timely notice of breach
   - Timely claim for damages (limitation period runs from breach date)

6. **Include Force Majeure Clauses**
   - Excuse performance for unforeseen events
   - Suspend liquidated damages during force majeure

### ❌ Don'ts

1. **Don't Use Excessive Penalty Clauses**
   - Unconscionable penalties reduced by courts (Section 74)
   - Stick to reasonable pre-estimates

2. **Don't Fail to Mitigate Losses**
   - Avoidable losses not recoverable
   - Cannot sit idle and let damages accumulate

3. **Don't Claim Remote/Speculative Losses**
   - Only foreseeable losses recoverable (Section 73)
   - Speculative future profits generally excluded

4. **Don't Assume Punitive Damages Available**
   - Contract law: Compensatory only (no punishment)
   - Exception: Tort actions for fraud may allow punitive damages

5. **Don't Delay Claiming Damages**
   - Limitation period (typically 3 years from breach date)
   - Undue delay may indicate waiver/acquiescence

6. **Don't Confuse Expectation, Reliance, and Restitution**
   - **Expectation**: Position if contract performed (Section 73 damages)
   - **Reliance**: Position if contract never made (quantum meruit, Section 75)
   - **Restitution**: Restore benefits (Sections 64-65)

---

## Related Provisions

### Within Indian Contract Act
- **Section 39** - Anticipatory breach
- **Section 55** - Time as essence (failure triggers voidability)
- **Section 56** - Impossibility excuses performance (no damages)
- **Section 64-65** - Restitution obligations
- **Section 75** - Damages upon rescission

### Cross-Domain Connections
- **Specific Relief Act, Section 10-22** - Specific performance remedy
- **Specific Relief Act, Section 36-42** - Injunctions
- **Sale of Goods Act, Section 55-61** - Damages in sale contracts (market price rule)
- **Limitation Act, 1963** - Time limits for breach of contract claims (Article 113: 3 years)
- **Interest Act, 1978** - Interest on damages

---

## Keywords
