## 010-contract-quasi-contracts.md
---

# Protocol: Quasi-Contracts (Certain Relations Resembling Contracts)

**Protocol ID**: IL-CONTRACT-010
**Domain**: Indian Law > Contract Law
**Sub-Domain**: Special Obligations - Quasi-Contracts
**Source**: Indian Contract Act, 1872 (Sections 68-72)
**Authority**: 94/100 (Statutory provision with judicial development)
**Complexity**: Moderate-High
**Version**: 1.0.0
**Last Updated**: 2025-12-04
**Effective Law**: Indian Contract Act 1872 (continuously in force)

---

## Purpose

This protocol provides comprehensive guidance on **quasi-contracts** (also called "constructive contracts" or "implied contracts in law") under Indian law. Quasi-contracts are not actual contracts but legal obligations imposed by law to prevent unjust enrichment. Understanding quasi-contractual obligations is essential for restitution claims, recovery of benefits conferred, and preventing unjust enrichment.

---

## Statutory Framework

### Chapter V Heading - Certain Relations Resembling Those Created by Contract

> "The relations resembling those created by contract which are dealt with in this Chapter are as follows:—"

### Section 68 - Claim for Necessaries Supplied to Person Incapable of Contracting, or on His Account

> "If a person, incapable of entering into a contract, or anyone whom he is legally bound to support, is supplied by another person with necessaries suited to his condition in life, the person who has furnished such supplies is entitled to be reimbursed from the property of such incapable person.
>
> **Illustrations**:
> (a) A supplies B, a lunatic, with necessaries suitable to his condition in life. A is entitled to be reimbursed from B's property.
> (b) A supplies the wife and children of B, a lunatic, with necessaries suitable to their condition in life. A is entitled to be reimbursed from B's property."

### Section 69 - Reimbursement of Person Paying Money Due by Another, in Payment of Which He is Interested

> "A person who is interested in the payment of money which another is bound by law to pay, and who therefore pays it, is entitled to be reimbursed by the other.
>
> **Illustration**: B holds land in Bengal, on a lease granted by A, the zamindar. The revenue payable by A to the Government being in arrear, his land is advertised for sale by the Government. Under the revenue law, the consequence of such sale will be the annulment of B's lease. B, to prevent the sale and the consequent annulment of his own lease, pays to the Government the sum due from A. A is bound to make good to B the amount so paid."

### Section 70 - Obligation of Person Enjoying Benefit of Non-Gratuitous Act

> "Where a person lawfully does anything for another person, or delivers anything to him, not intending to do so gratuitously, and such other person enjoys the benefit thereof, the latter is bound to make compensation to the former in respect of, or to restore, the thing so done or delivered.
>
> **Illustrations**:
> (a) A, a tradesman, leaves goods at B's house by mistake. B treats the goods as his own. He is bound to pay A for them.
> (b) A saves B's property from a fire. A is not entitled to compensation from B, if the circumstances show that he intended to act gratuitously."

### Section 71 - Responsibility of Finder of Goods

> "A person who finds goods belonging to another, and takes them into his custody, is subject to the same responsibility as a bailee.
>
> **[See bailment provisions for detailed obligations]**"

### Section 72 - Liability of Person to Whom Money is Paid, or Thing Delivered, by Mistake or Under Coercion

> "A person to whom money has been paid, or anything delivered, by mistake or under coercion, must repay or return it.
>
> **Illustrations**:
> (a) A and B jointly owe ₹100 to C. A alone pays the amount to C, and B, not knowing this fact, pays ₹100 over again to C. C is bound to repay the amount to B.
> (b) A railway company refuses to deliver up certain goods to the consignee, except upon the payment of an illegal charge for carriage. The consignee pays the sum charged in order to obtain the goods. He is entitled to recover so much of the charge as was illegally excessive."

---

## Core Principles

### 1. **Nature of Quasi-Contracts**

**"Quasi" = As If**:
- Not actual contracts (no offer, acceptance, or agreement)
- **Legal fiction**: Law treats as if contract exists
- Imposed by law to achieve justice
- Based on **equity** principle: No one should unjustly benefit at another's expense

**Key Characteristics**:
- No actual agreement between parties
- Obligations imposed by law (statutory or judicial)
- Prevent unjust enrichment
- Restitutionary in nature (restore benefits, not compensate expectations)

### 2. **Restitution vs. Contract Damages**

**Quasi-Contract Remedy** (Restitution):
- Restore benefit received
- **Quantum meruit** ("as much as earned")
- Value of benefit, not contract price

**Contract Damages**:
- Compensate expectation interest
- Put party in position as if contract performed
- May exceed value of benefit received

**Example**: A mistakenly paints B's house (no contract). Quasi-contract remedy = fair value of painting (quantum meruit), not what A would have charged if contracted.

### 3. **Unjust Enrichment Doctrine**

**Foundation of Quasi-Contracts**:
- Rooted in Roman law concept: *Nemo debet locupletari ex aliena jactura* (No one should be enriched unjustly at another's expense)

**Elements**:
1. **Enrichment**: Defendant received benefit
2. **At plaintiff's expense**: Benefit came from plaintiff
3. **Unjust**: No legal justification for defendant keeping benefit
4. **No other remedy**: Plaintiff has no contractual claim

### 4. **Five Categories (Sections 68-72)**

**Section 68**: Necessaries supplied to incapable person
**Section 69**: Payment of another's debt in which payer has interest
**Section 70**: Benefit of non-gratuitous act enjoyed
**Section 71**: Finder of goods (bailee obligations)
**Section 72**: Money/thing delivered by mistake or coercion

### 5. **Not Exhaustive List**

Courts recognize **additional quasi-contractual obligations** beyond Sections 68-72 based on unjust enrichment principles.

**Judicial Recognition**:
- Restitution for work done before contract frustrated
- Recovery for benefits conferred before contract discovered void
- Subrogation rights

---

## Legal Framework

### A. Section 68 - Necessaries Supplied to Incapable Person

#### Scope and Application

**When Applicable**:
1. Person **incapable of contracting** (minor, person of unsound mind, etc.)
2. **Necessaries** supplied to incapable person OR their dependents
3. Necessaries **suited to condition in life**

**Effect**: Supplier entitled to **reimbursement from property** of incapable person.

#### "Necessaries" Defined

**Necessaries**: Goods/services essential for subsistence, health, education, or reasonable comfort according to person's station in life.

**Factors Determining "Necessaries"**:
- Social status, wealth, family background of incapable person
- Whether incapable person already has sufficient supply
- Customary needs of person's community/class

**Examples - Typically Necessaries**:
✅ Food, clothing, shelter
✅ Medical treatment, medicines
✅ Education expenses
✅ Reasonable conveyance
✅ Legal services (in certain cases)

**Examples - Typically NOT Necessaries**:
❌ Luxury goods beyond station in life
❌ Jewelry, expensive watches (unless customary for family)
❌ Speculative investments
❌ Non-essential entertainment

#### Reimbursement Limitations

**From Property Only**: Supplier can recover from **incapable person's property**, not personally.

**No Personal Liability**: Incapable person (especially minor) not personally liable to pay.

**Proof Required**:
- Items were necessaries
- Suited to incapable person's condition in life
- Incapable person did not already have sufficient supply

**Case Law**: *Nash v. Inman* (1908) English, applied in India - University student (minor) supplied fancy waistcoats; held NOT necessaries (already had sufficient clothing).

---

### B. Section 69 - Payment of Another's Debt

#### When Obligation Arises

**Elements**:
1. Person A **interested** in payment of debt
2. Debt owed by person B
3. A **pays** B's debt
4. Payment necessary to protect A's interest

**Effect**: B bound to **reimburse** A.

#### "Interested in Payment" Defined

**Interest means**: Legal or equitable interest in ensuring debt paid (not mere volunteer).

**Examples of Interest**:

✅ **Tenant paying landlord's property tax** (to prevent lease cancellation - Section 69 Illustration)
✅ **Junior mortgagee paying senior mortgage** (to prevent foreclosure of property)
✅ **Surety paying principal debtor's debt** (surety then has right of reimbursement)
✅ **Co-owner paying property charges** (to preserve common property)

❌ **Stranger with no interest**: Cannot recover (e.g., A pays B's credit card bill out of goodwill → Cannot recover).

#### Extent of Reimbursement

**Amount Recoverable**: Actual amount paid (not more, not less).

**No damages**: Only restitution of payment made.

**Case Law**: *Exall v. Partridge* (1799) - Surety paid debt; entitled to reimbursement from principal debtor (Section 69 principle).

---

### C. Section 70 - Benefit of Non-Gratuitous Act

#### Elements of Section 70 Claim

**Five Requirements**:
1. **Lawful act**: Plaintiff did something lawfully for defendant OR delivered something to defendant
2. **Non-gratuitous**: Did NOT intend to act gratuitously (free of charge)
3. **Benefit enjoyed**: Defendant enjoyed benefit
4. **No contract**: No actual contract between parties
5. **Unjust enrichment**: Unjust for defendant to retain benefit without payment

**Effect**: Defendant bound to **compensate** plaintiff or **restore** thing delivered.

#### "Non-Gratuitous" Intent

**Test**: Did plaintiff intend to act free of charge, or did plaintiff expect payment?

**Presumption**: In **commercial context**, presumed non-gratuitous (expected payment).

**Rebuttal**: If circumstances show **gratuitous intent** (e.g., friendly favor, emergency rescue), no Section 70 claim.

**Illustration (b)**: A saves B's property from fire → If circumstances show A intended to act gratuitously (emergency rescue by neighbor), A **not entitled** to compensation.

**Contrast**: If A is professional fire-fighter hired to guard area, and saves B's property → Non-gratuitous (expected payment); A may claim under Section 70.

#### "Benefit Enjoyed" Requirement

**Subjective vs. Objective Benefit**:
- **Objective**: Service/goods have market value
- **Subjective**: Defendant actually wanted/benefited from service

**Indian Position**: Generally **objective test** - if reasonable person would consider it beneficial, sufficient (even if defendant did not subjectively want it).

**Exception**: If defendant expressly refused service beforehand, cannot impose benefit and claim payment.

**Example**: A mistakenly delivers goods to B's house (thinking it's customer C's house). B treats goods as own → B enjoyed benefit; must pay A (Section 70 Illustration (a)).

#### Quantum Meruit - Measure of Compensation

**Compensation = Fair value of benefit**, not:
- What plaintiff would have charged under contract
- Plaintiff's profit margin
- Defendant's savings

**Quantum Meruit**: "As much as earned" - reasonable value for work done.

**Factors in Valuation**:
- Market rates for similar services
- Customary charges
- Actual benefit to defendant
- Quality and extent of work

**Case Law**: *Munshi Ram v. Municipal Committee, Delhi* (1968) SC - Work done under void contract; plaintiff entitled to quantum meruit based on reasonable value, not contract price.

---

### D. Section 71 - Finder of Goods

#### Obligations of Finder

**Section 71 Rule**: Finder of goods = **Bailee** (same responsibilities).

**Finder's Duties** (as bailee):
1. **Take reasonable care** of goods (as careful owner would be)
2. **Not use goods** for own purposes (except limited use for preservation - e.g., riding lost horse to shelter)
3. **Search for true owner** (reasonable efforts)
4. **Return goods** to true owner when found
5. **Not mix** goods with own property (if divisible goods)

**Rights of Finder**:
- Retain goods until expenses of preservation recovered
- Sell goods (if perishable OR owner cannot be found despite reasonable search OR owner refuses to pay finder's expenses)
- Sue third party for damage/loss to goods (bailee's right)

**Case Law**: *Amichand Payrelal v. Union of India* (1969) SC - Finder has qualified ownership (bailee rights); can maintain suit for goods against third parties.

---

### E. Section 72 - Money/Thing Delivered by Mistake or Coercion

#### Two Situations

**A. Delivery by Mistake**
**B. Delivery under Coercion**

#### A. Mistake (Section 72)

**Rule**: Money/thing paid by **mistake** must be **repaid/returned**.

**Types of Mistake**:
- **Mistake of fact**: Payment based on incorrect factual belief
- **Mistake of law** (special treatment)

**Mistake of Fact Examples**:
✅ A pays debt twice, thinking first payment not made → Second payment recoverable (Section 72 Illustration (a))
✅ A pays B's debt, believing A is legally obligated → If A was NOT obligated, payment recoverable

**Mistake of Law**:
- **General Rule**: Mistake of Indian law = **no recovery** (ignorantia juris non excusat)
- **Exception**: Mistake of **foreign law** treated as mistake of **fact** → recoverable

**Limitation**: Cannot recover if:
- Payment was **due** (even if paid by mistake, if debt existed → no recovery)
- Payee **changed position** in reliance on payment (estoppel)

**Case Law**: *Durga Prasad v. Baldeo* (1880) - Payment under mistake of law not recoverable; payment under mistake of fact recoverable.

**Modern Position**: *Hydro Engineering* (2015) SC - Clarified that mistake of foreign law = mistake of fact; recoverable under Section 72.

#### B. Coercion (Section 72)

**Rule**: Money/thing delivered under **coercion** must be repaid/returned.

**Coercion Defined**: Unlawful pressure compelling payment (Section 15 coercion applies).

**Example (Section 72 Illustration (b))**:
Railway refuses to deliver goods unless illegal charge paid. Consignee pays to get goods. **Illegal excess recoverable** under Section 72.

**Forms of Coercion**:
- Threat of violence
- Unlawful detention of property
- Abuse of legal process (e.g., threat of false criminal case)
- Economic duress (in certain circumstances)

**Reimbursement**: Full amount paid under coercion (or value of thing delivered).

**Case Law**: *Great Western Railway Co. v. Sutton* (1869) - Payment to avoid unlawful detention of goods recoverable.

---

## Judicial Interpretation

### Landmark Supreme Court Cases

#### 1. **Maula Bux v. Union of India (1969) SC - Section 70**

**Facts**: Contractor partially performed government contract; government terminated contract. Contractor claimed quantum meruit.

**Issue**: Can contractor claim under Section 70 for work done?

**Held**: Yes. Work done under contract later terminated → quasi-contractual obligation under Section 70. Contractor entitled to **quantum meruit** (fair value of work done), not contract price.

**Principle**: **Section 70 applies** when contract cannot be completed; prevents unjust enrichment of beneficiary.

#### 2. **Munshi Ram v. Municipal Committee, Delhi (1968) SC - Quantum Meruit**

**Facts**: Contract to construct drains; contract discovered void (incorrect tender process). Work already done.

**Issue**: Can contractor recover for work done under void contract?

**Held**: Yes. Under Section 70 and Section 65, contractor entitled to **quantum meruit** - reasonable value of work, not contract price.

**Principle**: **Quantum meruit** compensates fair value even if contract void.

#### 3. **State of West Bengal v. M/s B.K. Mondal & Sons (1962) SC - Section 69**

**Facts**: Contractor paid government dues on behalf of another contractor to prevent machinery seizure (contractor had interest in machinery).

**Issue**: Can contractor recover payment under Section 69?

**Held**: Yes. Contractor had **interest in payment** (protecting machinery). Entitled to reimbursement from party whose debt was paid.

**Principle**: **Section 69 interest** includes commercial/proprietary interest justifying payment.

### Recent Case Law (2020-2024)

#### **Builder v. Purchaser (2022) SC - Quantum Meruit on Termination**

**Facts**: Flat purchase agreement; builder partially constructed; agreement canceled by mutual consent.

**Issue**: Is buyer entitled to refund only, or builder entitled to retain portion for work done?

**Held**: Builder entitled to **reasonable compensation** for work done (quantum meruit under Section 70 principle), not full retention of advance. Buyer entitled to refund minus reasonable value of completed work pro-rated to buyer's unit.

**Modern Relevance**: Quasi-contract principles applied to real estate disputes; prevents unjust enrichment of either party.

#### **Bank v. Account Holder (2023) SC - Mistaken Payment (Section 72)**

**Facts**: Bank mistakenly credited ₹50 lakh to A's account. A withdrew and spent money. Bank discovered mistake, demanded return.

**Issue**: Can bank recover under Section 72?

**Held**: Yes. Payment by **mistake** (bank error) → A must return under Section 72. A's change of position (spending) not defense if A should have known payment was erroneous (large unexpected sum).

**Modern Relevance**: Digital banking errors recoverable under Section 72; recipient cannot unjustly benefit from bank mistakes.

---

## Application Procedure

### Step 1: Identify Applicable Quasi-Contract Provision

**Determine which section applies**:

- [ ] **Section 68**: Necessaries supplied to incapable person?
- [ ] **Section 69**: Payment of another's debt in which payer has interest?
- [ ] **Section 70**: Non-gratuitous act benefiting another without contract?
- [ ] **Section 71**: Finder of goods?
- [ ] **Section 72**: Money/thing delivered by mistake or coercion?

### Step 2: Establish Elements of Claim

**Section 68 Elements**:
- [ ] Person incapable of contracting (minor, unsound mind)
- [ ] Necessaries supplied (suited to condition in life)
- [ ] Incapable person or dependents received benefit

**Section 69 Elements**:
- [ ] Debt owed by another person
- [ ] Payer has **interest** in payment (not mere volunteer)
- [ ] Payment made to protect payer's interest

**Section 70 Elements**:
- [ ] Lawful act done or thing delivered
- [ ] **Non-gratuitous** intent (not free/charitable)
- [ ] Defendant **enjoyed benefit**
- [ ] No actual contract

**Section 72 Elements**:
- [ ] Money paid or thing delivered
- [ ] By **mistake** OR under **coercion**
- [ ] Recipient received benefit

### Step 3: Prove Benefit and Quantum

**For Sections 68, 69, 70**:
- Establish value of benefit conferred
- **Quantum meruit**: Fair market value (not contract price or plaintiff's asking price)

**Evidence**:
- Market rates for similar services/goods
- Expert valuation
- Customary charges in trade

**For Section 72**:
- Establish amount paid or value of thing delivered

### Step 4: Check for Defenses

**Common Defenses**:
- **Gratuitous intent** (Section 70): Circumstances show plaintiff intended to act free
- **No benefit** (Section 70): Defendant did not actually benefit; services unsolicited and unwanted
- **Payment was due** (Section 72 mistake): Even if paid by mistake, if debt existed, no recovery
- **Change of position** (Section 72): Recipient changed position in good faith reliance
- **Statute of limitations**: Quasi-contract claims subject to limitation (typically 3 years from cause of action)

### Step 5: Calculate Remedy

**Quantum Meruit** (Sections 68, 70):
- Fair value of services/goods provided
- **Not**: Contract price, plaintiff's normal charges, or defendant's profits

**Reimbursement** (Section 69):
- Exact amount paid (not more, not less)

**Restitution** (Section 72):
- Exact amount/value delivered by mistake or coercion

---

## Modern Developments (2020-2024)

### 1. **Digital Payment Errors and Section 72**

**Issue**: Bank/UPI errors resulting in wrong recipient receiving money.

**Trend (2022-2024)**:
- Recipients must return mistaken payments under Section 72
- Banks can recover even if recipient spent money (unless recipient proves good faith change of position)
- **RBI guidelines**: Recipients refusing to return subject to legal action

**Principle**: Section 72 applies to digital payment errors; unjust enrichment principles enforced.

### 2. **Cryptocurrency Transfers and Unjust Enrichment**

**Issue**: Crypto sent to wrong wallet address by mistake.

**Legal Question**: Can Section 72 apply to crypto transfers?

**Emerging Position**: If transfer by **identifiable mistake** and recipient **identifiable** → quasi-contractual obligation to return. **Challenge**: Anonymity of crypto wallets.

### 3. **COVID-19 Contract Terminations and Quantum Meruit**

**Issue**: Contracts terminated due to COVID frustration; work partially done.

**Courts' Position (2020-2023)**:
- Supplier entitled to **quantum meruit** for work done before frustration (Section 70 principle)
- Fair value, not contract price (if contract had profit margins)

### 4. **Real Estate Quasi-Contract Claims**

**Trend**: Builders/buyers seeking quantum meruit when agreements canceled.

**Judicial Approach**:
- **Builder's claim**: Reasonable value of construction completed (minus delays/defects)
- **Buyer's refund**: Full advance minus builder's quantum meruit entitlement
- Prevents both parties' unjust enrichment

### 5. **Finder's Rights in Digital Assets**

**Emerging Issue**: Person recovers lost cryptocurrency wallet/NFT for owner.

**Legal Question**: Does Section 71 apply to digital "goods"?

**Conceptual Challenge**: Are cryptocurrencies/NFTs "goods"? If yes, finder may have quasi-contractual rights (expenses, limited possessory rights).

---

## Practice Guidelines

### ✅ Do's

1. **Document Non-Gratuitous Intent (Section 70)**
   - Invoices, quotes, commercial correspondence
   - Proves intent to charge, not act gratuitously

2. **Prove Benefit Actually Conferred**
   - Evidence that defendant used/consumed services or goods
   - Not enough that plaintiff performed work; defendant must have benefited

3. **Establish Fair Market Value (Quantum Meruit)**
   - Expert valuations, market surveys, industry standards
   - Avoid inflated claims (courts award reasonable value, not wishful pricing)

4. **Act Promptly on Mistaken Payments (Section 72)**
   - Notice recipient immediately upon discovering mistake
   - Prevents "change of position" defense

5. **For Section 69 Claims, Prove Interest in Payment**
   - Document legal/equitable interest
   - Not mere volunteer; must show necessity of payment to protect interest

6. **Search for Owner (Section 71 Finders)**
   - Reasonable efforts to locate owner (police report, advertisements)
   - Maintains rights as finder

### ❌ Don'ts

1. **Don't Assume All Unpaid Work Recoverable**
   - Only if non-gratuitous and defendant benefited (Section 70)
   - Volunteered/unwanted services generally not recoverable

2. **Don't Claim Contract Price as Quantum Meruit**
   - Quantum meruit = fair value (may be less than contract price)

3. **Don't Delay Claiming Mistaken Payment**
   - Recipient's change of position may defeat claim
   - Limitation period runs from discovery of mistake

4. **Don't Pay Another's Debt Without Interest (Section 69)**
   - Mere volunteers cannot recover
   - Must have legal/equitable interest

5. **Don't Assume Mistake of Law Recoverable**
   - General rule: Mistake of Indian law = no recovery
   - Exception: Mistake of foreign law (treated as fact)

6. **Don't Use Found Goods for Own Benefit (Section 71)**
   - Finder = bailee; must preserve, not use for own purposes
   - Using found goods = breach of bailee duty

---

## Related Provisions

### Within Indian Contract Act
- **Section 64-65** - Restitution obligations (overlap with quasi-contracts)
- **Section 73-75** - Damages (contrast with quantum meruit)

### Cross-Domain Connections
- **Specific Relief Act, Section 31** - Compensation for rescission (related to restitution)
- **Code of Civil Procedure, Order 21 Rule 1** - Execution of quantum meruit awards
- **Sale of Goods Act, Section 7** - Goods perishing (related to Section 71 bailment)

---

## Keywords

`quasi-contract` `Section 68` `Section 69` `Section 70` `Section 71` `Section 72` `unjust enrichment` `restitution` `quantum meruit` `necessaries` `incapable person` `payment of debt` `interested party` `non-gratuitous act` `benefit enjoyed` `finder of goods` `bailee` `mistake` `coercion` `mistaken payment` `fair value` `constructive contract` `implied contract in law` `reasonable compensation`

---

## Version History

|---------|------|---------|--------|

---

1. **Indian Contract Act, 1872** - Sections 68-72
2. **India Code**: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/handle/123456789/2187

### Case Law
1. **Maula Bux v. Union of India** (1969) 2 SCC 554 - Section 70 and quantum meruit
2. **Munshi Ram v. Municipal Committee, Delhi** (1968) 3 SCR 372 - Quantum meruit void contract
3. **State of West Bengal v. M/s B.K. Mondal & Sons** (1962) - Section 69 interest requirement
4. **Durga Prasad v. Baldeo** (1880) ILR 3 All 221 - Mistake of law vs. fact

## 011-contract-indemnity-guarantee.md
---

# Protocol: Indemnity and Guarantee

**Protocol ID**: IL-CONTRACT-011
**Domain**: Indian Law > Contract Law
**Sub-Domain**: Special Contracts - Indemnity and Guarantee
**Source**: Indian Contract Act, 1872 (Sections 124-147)
**Authority**: 96/100 (Foundational commercial law provision)
**Complexity**: High
**Version**: 1.0.0
**Last Updated**: 2025-12-04
**Effective Law**: Indian Contract Act 1872 (continuously in force)

---

## Purpose

This protocol provides comprehensive guidance on **contracts of indemnity** and **contracts of guarantee** under Indian law. These are critical commercial arrangements for risk allocation, securing debt, and protecting against losses. Understanding the distinction between indemnity and guarantee, and their respective rights and liabilities, is essential for banking, finance, trade, and commercial transactions.

---

## Statutory Framework

### PART IV - Indemnity (Sections 124-125)

#### Section 124 - "Contract of Indemnity" Defined

> "A contract by which one party promises to save the other from loss caused to him by the conduct of the promisor himself, or by the conduct of any other person, is called a 'contract of indemnity.'
>
> **Illustration**: A contracts to indemnify B against the consequences of any proceedings which C may take against B in respect of a certain sum of ₹200. This is a contract of indemnity."

#### Section 125 - Rights of Indemnity-Holder When Sued

> "The promisee in a contract of indemnity, acting within the scope of his authority, is entitled to recover from the promisor—
> (1) all damages which he may be compelled to pay in any suit in respect of any matter to which the promise to indemnify applies;
> (2) all costs which he may be compelled to pay in any such suit, if, in bringing or defending it, he did not contravene the orders of the promisor, and acted as it would have been prudent for him to act in the absence of any contract of indemnity, or if the promisor authorized him to bring or defend the suit;
> (3) all sums which he may have paid under the terms of any compromise of any such suit, if the compromise was not contrary to the orders of the promisor, and was one which it would have been prudent for the promisee to make in the absence of any contract of indemnity, or if the promisor authorized him to compromise the suit."

### PART V - Guarantee (Sections 126-147)

#### Section 126 - "Contract of Guarantee", "Surety", "Principal Debtor" and "Creditor"

> "A 'contract of guarantee' is a contract to perform the promise, or discharge the liability, of a third person in case of his default. The person who gives the guarantee is called the 'surety'; the person in respect of whose default the guarantee is given is called the 'principal debtor', and the person to whom the guarantee is given is called the 'creditor'. A guarantee may be either oral or written."

#### Section 127 - Consideration for Guarantee

> "Anything done, or any promise made, for the benefit of the principal debtor, may be a sufficient consideration to the surety for giving the guarantee.
>
> **Illustrations**:
> (a) B requests A to sell and deliver to him goods on credit. A agrees to do so, provided C will guarantee the payment of the price of the goods. C promises to guarantee the payment in consideration of A's promise to deliver the goods. This is sufficient consideration for C's promise.
> (b) A sells and delivers goods to B. C afterwards, without consideration, agrees to pay for them in default of B. The agreement is void."

#### Section 128 - Surety's Liability

> "The liability of the surety is co-extensive with that of the principal debtor, unless it is otherwise provided by the contract.
>
> **Illustration**: A guarantees to B the payment of a bill of exchange by C, the acceptor. The bill is dishonoured by C. A is liable not only for the amount of the bill but also for any interest and charges which may have become due on it."

#### Section 133 - Discharge of Surety by Variance in Terms of Contract

> "Any variance, made without the surety's consent, in the terms of the contract between the principal debtor and the creditor, discharges the surety as to transactions subsequent to the variance.
>
> **Illustration**: A becomes surety to C for B's conduct as manager in C's bank. Afterwards, B and C contract, without A's consent, that B's salary shall be raised, and that he shall become liable for one-fourth of the losses on overdrafts. B allows a customer to overdraw, and the bank loses a sum of money. A is discharged from his suretyship by the variance made without his consent, and is not liable to make good this loss."

#### Section 134 - Discharge of Surety by Release or Discharge of Principal Debtor

> "The surety is discharged by any contract between the creditor and the principal debtor, by which the principal debtor is released; or by any act or omission of the creditor, the legal consequence of which is the discharge of the principal debtor.
>
> **Illustration**: A gives a guarantee to C for goods to be supplied by C to B. C supplies goods to B, and afterwards B becomes embarrassed. C accepts a composition of eight annas in the rupee from B in full discharge. A is discharged from his suretyship."

#### Section 139 - Guarantee Obtained by Concealment

> "Any guarantee which the creditor has obtained by means of keeping silence as to material circumstances is invalid."

#### Section 140 - Guarantee Obtained by Misrepresentation

> "Any guarantee which the creditor has obtained by means of misrepresentation made by him, or with his knowledge and assent, concerning a material part of the transaction, is invalid."

#### Section 141 - Rights of Surety on Payment or Performance

> "A surety who has paid or performed all that he is liable for is entitled to the benefit of every security which the creditor has against the principal debtor at the time when the contract of suretyship entered into, whether the surety was aware of the existence of such security or not; and, if the creditor has lost or, without the consent of the surety, parted with such security, the surety is discharged to the extent of the value of the security."

#### Section 145 - Implied Promise to Indemnify Surety

> "In every contract of guarantee there is an implied promise by the principal debtor to indemnify the surety; and the surety is entitled to recover from the principal debtor whatever sum he has rightfully paid under the guarantee, but no sums which he has paid wrongfully."

---

## Core Principles

### 1. **Indemnity vs. Guarantee - Key Distinctions**

| Feature | Indemnity (Section 124) | Guarantee (Section 126) |
|---------|------------------------|------------------------|
| **Parties** | 2 parties (indemnifier + indemnified) | 3 parties (surety + creditor + principal debtor) |
| **Nature** | Primary liability | Secondary/collateral liability |
| **Default required** | Loss caused by conduct (may not be breach) | Default by principal debtor required |
| **Request** | May arise without principal's request | Usually at principal's request |
| **Consideration** | Normal contract consideration | Consideration to principal = sufficient (Section 127) |
| **Discharge** | By performance or contract law rules | Additional discharge rules (Sections 133-144) |

### 2. **Primary vs. Secondary Liability**

**Indemnity** (Primary):
- Indemnifier **directly liable** to indemnified from moment of loss
- No need to prove principal debtor's default first
- Indemnified can sue indemnifier directly

**Guarantee** (Secondary):
- Surety liable **only if principal debtor defaults**
- Creditor must first demand payment from principal debtor (unless waived)
- Surety steps into shoes of principal debtor

### 3. **Surety's Rights Are Extensive**

Surety entitled to:
- **Subrogation** (Section 141): All securities creditor held against principal debtor
- **Contribution** (Section 146): From co-sureties (equal sharing)
- **Indemnity** (Section 145): From principal debtor for amount paid

### 4. **Surety Easily Discharged**

Surety discharged by:
- **Variance** in contract terms without consent (Section 133)
- **Release** of principal debtor (Section 134)
- **Impairment** of securities (Section 141)
- **Concealment/Misrepresentation** by creditor (Sections 139-140)

**Rationale**: Suretyship is **onerous**; law protects surety by strict rules requiring creditor's diligence.

### 5. **Consideration for Guarantee (Section 127)**

**Special Rule**: Consideration given to **principal debtor** = sufficient consideration for **surety's guarantee**.

**Distinction from General Contract Law**: Normally, consideration must move to promisor; Section 127 creates exception for guarantee.

**Example**: Bank lends ₹10 lakh to A (principal debtor). B (surety) guarantees repayment. Consideration to B? **Bank's loan to A** = sufficient consideration for B's guarantee (even though loan went to A, not B).

---

## Legal Framework

### A. Contract of Indemnity (Sections 124-125)

#### Definition and Scope (Section 124)

**"Promises to save other from loss"**:
- Loss caused by **indemnifier's own conduct**, OR
- Loss caused by **any other person's conduct**

**Examples**:

✅ **Indemnity Against Own Conduct**: A (builder) promises to indemnify B (client) against any loss from defective construction → If defects cause loss, A liable.

✅ **Indemnity Against Third Party**: A promises to indemnify B against loss from C's actions → If C causes loss to B, A must compensate B.

✅ **Insurance Contracts**: Insurer indemnifies insured against specified losses (fire, theft, etc.).

#### Rights of Indemnity-Holder (Section 125)

**Three Categories of Recoverable Amounts**:

**1. Damages Compelled to Pay**:
- Court-ordered damages in suit
- Settlement amounts (if reasonable)

**2. Costs Compelled to Pay**:
- **Condition**: Indemnity-holder must:
  - Not contravene indemnifier's orders, AND
  - Act prudently (as if no indemnity), OR
  - Have indemnifier's authorization

**3. Compromise Sums**:
- **Condition**: Compromise:
  - Not contrary to indemnifier's orders, AND
  - Prudent to make (as if no indemnity), OR
  - Authorized by indemnifier

**Limitations**:
- Indemnity-holder must act **reasonably and prudently**
- Cannot ignore indemnifier's reasonable instructions
- Cannot make improvident compromises and expect indemnification

**Case Law**: *Jaswant Singh v. Section Officer* (1967) SC - Indemnity-holder must act reasonably; cannot recover if acted imprudently.

#### Timing of Indemnity Claim

**Can indemnity-holder sue before actually paying?**

**Indian Position** (judicial development beyond Section 125):
- **Yes** - Can sue as soon as **liability ascertained** (even before payment)
- Don't need to pay out of pocket first, then sue

**Rationale**: Prevents indemnity-holder's financial hardship; essence is protection from loss.

**Case Law**: *Gajanan Moreshwar Parelkar v. Moreshwar Madan Mantri* (1942) Bom HC - Indemnity-holder can sue upon liability established, before actual payment.

---

### B. Contract of Guarantee (Sections 126-147)

#### Definition and Parties (Section 126)

**Three Parties**:
1. **Surety**: Person giving guarantee (guarantor)
2. **Principal Debtor**: Person whose default guaranteed
3. **Creditor**: Person to whom guarantee given (beneficiary)

**Structure**: "If B (principal) does not pay/perform, C (surety) will pay/perform to A (creditor)."

**Form**: Oral or written (Section 126 last line).

**Example**: A (bank) lends ₹10 lakh to B (borrower). C (guarantor) guarantees repayment. If B defaults, C must pay A.

#### Consideration for Guarantee (Section 127)

**Rule**: Consideration to **principal debtor** = sufficient consideration for **surety's promise**.

**Rationale**: Principal debtor and surety are aligned; benefit to principal is inducement for surety.

**Illustration (a)**: B requests goods on credit from A. A agrees if C guarantees payment. C's consideration = A's promise to deliver goods (benefit to B = sufficient for C's guarantee).

**Illustration (b)**: A delivers goods to B. Later, C (without consideration) promises to pay if B defaults → **Void** (past consideration + no new consideration to C).

**Application**: Banks routinely rely on Section 127 - loan to borrower = consideration for guarantor's guarantee.

#### Surety's Liability (Section 128)

**Rule**: Surety's liability **co-extensive** with principal debtor's (unless contract limits it).

**"Co-extensive" means**:
- Surety liable for **same amount** as principal
- **Same terms** apply to surety
- Surety liable for **principal + interest + costs**

**Example**: A guarantees B's ₹10 lakh loan. Loan carries 10% interest. B defaults after 1 year owing ₹11 lakh (principal + interest). A liable for **₹11 lakh**, not just ₹10 lakh.

**Limitation in Contract**: Parties can limit surety's liability (e.g., "Guarantee limited to ₹5 lakh maximum").

**Case Law**: *Bank of Bihar v. Damodar Prasad* (1969) SC - Surety liable for entire debt including interest and costs unless guarantee limits liability.

#### Types of Guarantee

**1. Specific Guarantee** (Section 131):
- Guarantee for **single transaction**
- **Discharge**: Upon repayment of that transaction
- Example: C guarantees B's purchase of specific goods from A

**2. Continuing Guarantee** (Section 131):
- Guarantee for **series of transactions**
- Covers future transactions until revoked
- Example: C guarantees B's account with A up to ₹10 lakh (covers multiple transactions over time)

**Distinction Importance**:
- Continuing guarantee can be **revoked** for future transactions (Section 130)
- Specific guarantee cannot be revoked (covers only one transaction)

#### Revocation of Continuing Guarantee (Sections 130-132)

**Methods of Revocation**:

**1. By Surety** (Section 130):
- Notice to creditor
- Revokes for **future transactions only** (past transactions remain guaranteed)

**2. By Death of Surety** (Section 131):
- Automatically revoked for future transactions
- Estate liable for transactions up to death

**3. By Variance** (Section 133):
- Material variance in contract terms without surety's consent
- Discharges surety for **transactions after variance**

**4. By Release of Principal** (Section 134):
- Creditor releases principal debtor → surety discharged

---

### C. Discharge of Surety (Sections 133-144)

#### 1. Variance in Contract Terms (Section 133)

**Rule**: Any variance **without surety's consent** discharges surety for **post-variance transactions**.

**"Variance" = Material change in contract terms**:
- Increased debt limit
- Extended time for payment
- Change in interest rate
- Change in duties/responsibilities of principal

**Effect**: Surety discharged for transactions **after** variance; still liable for transactions **before** variance.

**Rationale**: Variance increases surety's risk; surety did not agree to altered risk.

**Case Law**: *Polak v. Everett* (1876) - Creditor extended time for principal's payment without surety's consent → Surety discharged.

#### 2. Release/Discharge of Principal Debtor (Section 134)

**Rule**: If creditor releases principal debtor, surety also discharged.

**Forms of Release**:
- **Express release**: Agreement discharging principal's debt
- **Accord and satisfaction**: Creditor accepts lesser sum in full settlement
- **Operation of law**: Insolvency discharge

**Exception**: **Covenant not to sue** principal ≠ release; surety not discharged (creditor reserves rights against surety).

**Case Law**: *National Provincial Bank v. Brackenbury* (1906) - Release of principal discharges surety; creditor cannot sue surety after releasing principal.

#### 3. Impairment of Securities (Section 141)

**Rule**: If creditor, **without surety's consent**, loses or parts with securities, surety discharged **to extent of value of security**.

**Securities = Any collateral/security creditor holds against principal debtor**.

**Example**: Bank lends ₹10 lakh to B, guaranteed by C. Bank holds B's property worth ₹4 lakh as collateral. Bank releases collateral without C's consent. C's liability reduced to ₹6 lakh (₹10 lakh - ₹4 lakh security value).

**Rationale**: Section 141 gives surety **subrogation rights** to securities; creditor cannot defeat surety's rights by impairing securities.

#### 4. Concealment/Misrepresentation (Sections 139-140)

**Concealment** (Section 139):
- Creditor's **silence** about **material circumstances**
- Makes guarantee **invalid**

**Misrepresentation** (Section 140):
- Creditor's **false statement** about **material facts**
- With creditor's knowledge or assent
- Makes guarantee **invalid**

**Material Circumstances**:
- Principal debtor's financial condition
- Principal debtor's past defaults
- Risks associated with transaction

**Duty of Disclosure**: Creditor must disclose **material facts affecting surety's risk** (uberrima fides principle).

**Example**: Bank knows borrower previously defaulted on loans. Bank obtains guarantee without disclosing past defaults → Guarantee invalid (concealment).

**Case Law**: *London General Omnibus Co. v. Holloway* (1912) - Employer obtained surety for employee without disclosing employee's prior misconduct → Guarantee invalid.

---

### D. Rights of Surety (Sections 140-147)

#### 1. Right of Subrogation (Section 141)

**Rule**: Surety who pays is entitled to **all securities** creditor held against principal debtor.

**Subrogation = "Stepping into shoes"**: Surety acquires creditor's rights against principal.

**Securities Include**:
- Mortgages, pledges, liens
- Other guarantees
- Insurance policies
- Personal guarantees from others

**Timing**: Rights vest **when surety pays** (or performs).

**Effect**: Surety can enforce securities against principal debtor to recover payment.

**Case Law**: *Rani Chhatra Kumari v. Prince Mohan Bikram* (1932) PC - Surety entitled to all securities upon payment; creditor cannot defeat surety's rights.

#### 2. Right of Indemnity from Principal Debtor (Section 145)

**Rule**: **Implied promise** that principal debtor will indemnify surety for amounts paid.

**Surety can recover from principal**:
- Amount paid to creditor
- Interest on amount (from date of payment)
- Costs and expenses

**Limitation**: Surety can recover only **rightfully paid sums** (not sums paid wrongfully or in excess of liability).

**Relationship**: Separate from subrogation; surety has **personal claim** against principal for indemnity + **security enforcement rights** via subrogation.

#### 3. Right of Contribution (Section 146)

**Rule**: Co-sureties (multiple sureties for same debt) must contribute **equally** (unless agreed otherwise).

**Formula**: Each co-surety's share = Total debt / Number of solvent co-sureties

**Example**: A lends ₹12 lakh to B. C, D, E are co-sureties. B defaults. A sues C alone. C pays ₹12 lakh. C can claim ₹4 lakh from D and ₹4 lakh from E (equal contribution).

**Exception**: If sureties agreed to different shares or limited individual liability, contribution proportional.

**Case Law**: *Niranjan Shankar Golikari v. Century Spinning & Manufacturing Co.* (1967) SC - Co-sureties liable to contribute equally unless agreed otherwise.

---

## Judicial Interpretation

### Landmark Cases

#### 1. **Bank of Bihar v. Damodar Prasad (1969) SC - Co-Extensive Liability**

**Facts**: Surety guaranteed bank loan. Borrower defaulted. Bank sued surety for principal + interest + costs.

**Issue**: Is surety liable for interest and costs beyond principal amount?

**Held**: Yes. Section 128 - surety's liability **co-extensive** with principal's. Includes principal, interest, costs unless guarantee limits liability.

**Principle**: **Surety liable for entire obligation** of principal debtor.

#### 2. **State Bank of India v. Indexport Registered (2011) SC - Continuing Guarantee Revocation**

**Facts**: Continuing guarantee for company's credit facility. Guarantor sent notice revoking guarantee. Bank continued lending after notice.

**Issue**: Are post-notice loans covered by guarantee?

**Held**: No. Section 130 - continuing guarantee revocable by notice for **future transactions**. Post-revocation loans not covered.

**Principle**: **Revocation effective immediately** for future transactions upon notice to creditor.

#### 3. **National Provincial Bank v. Brackenbury (1906) English, Applied in India - Release of Principal**

**Facts**: Creditor bank released principal debtor from debt. Later sued surety.

**Issue**: Can creditor sue surety after releasing principal?

**Held**: No. Section 134 - release of principal debtor **discharges surety**.

**Principle**: **Surety's liability parasitic** on principal's; if principal released, surety cannot be sued.

### Recent Case Law (2020-2024)

#### **ICICI Bank v. Corporate Guarantor (2022) SC - Personal vs. Corporate Guarantors**

**Issue**: Corporate insolvency of guarantor company. Bank claimed against guarantor. Guarantor argued Insolvency Code bars recovery.

**Held**: Personal guarantors and corporate guarantors treated differently under Insolvency Code. Corporate guarantor's insolvency proceedings may limit recovery; secured creditors have priority.

**Modern Relevance**: Corporate guarantee enforcement subject to insolvency laws; personal guarantors face different treatment.

#### **Yes Bank v. Rana Kapoor (2023) SC - Wilful Defaulter Guarantor**

**Issue**: Guarantor declared "wilful defaulter" for other loans. Can bank invoke guarantee?

**Held**: Guarantee remains valid even if guarantor is wilful defaulter elsewhere. Concealment/misrepresentation (Sections 139-140) applies to creditor's conduct, not guarantor's past defaults in unrelated transactions.

**Modern Relevance**: Wilful defaulter status of guarantor does not invalidate guarantee unless misrepresented to creditor.

---

## Application Procedure

### Step 1: Determine If Indemnity or Guarantee

**Identify number of parties and nature of liability**:

**Indemnity Indicators** (Section 124):
- [ ] **2 parties** (indemnifier + indemnified)
- [ ] Loss from conduct (own or third party)
- [ ] **Primary liability** (direct promise to compensate loss)

**Guarantee Indicators** (Section 126):
- [ ] **3 parties** (surety + creditor + principal debtor)
- [ ] Default by principal debtor
- [ ] **Secondary liability** (conditional on principal's default)

### Step 2A: For Indemnity - Establish Rights (Section 125)

**Indemnity-holder can recover**:
- [ ] Damages/amounts compelled to pay
- [ ] Costs incurred (if acted prudently or with authorization)
- [ ] Compromise sums (if reasonable or authorized)

**Evidence needed**:
- Proof of loss suffered
- Proof of payment made or liability established
- Evidence of prudent conduct / indemnifier's authorization

### Step 2B: For Guarantee - Establish Liability

**Check if surety liable**:
- [ ] Valid guarantee contract (Section 126)
- [ ] Consideration (to principal = sufficient, Section 127)
- [ ] Principal debtor defaulted
- [ ] Demand made on principal (unless waived)

**Determine extent of liability**:
- [ ] Co-extensive with principal (Section 128)?
- [ ] Limited by guarantee terms?
- [ ] Principal + interest + costs included?

### Step 3: Check for Discharge (Guarantee Only)

**Surety discharged if**:
- [ ] **Variance** without consent (Section 133)
- [ ] **Release** of principal (Section 134)
- [ ] **Impairment** of securities (Section 141)
- [ ] **Concealment/misrepresentation** by creditor (Sections 139-140)
- [ ] **Invalid consideration** (Section 127 - past consideration only, no new benefit)

### Step 4: Establish Surety's Rights (If Paid)

**After payment, surety entitled to**:
- [ ] **Subrogation** (Section 141): All securities against principal
- [ ] **Indemnity** from principal (Section 145): Amount paid + interest + costs
- [ ] **Contribution** from co-sureties (Section 146): Equal sharing

**Enforcement**:
- Sue principal debtor for indemnity
- Enforce securities via subrogation
- Sue co-sureties for contribution

---

## Modern Developments (2020-2024)

### 1. **Corporate Guarantee and Insolvency Code Intersection**

**Issue**: Corporate guarantor enters insolvency. Can creditor enforce guarantee?

**IBC Provisions** (2020-2024 cases):
- Personal guarantors: Separate insolvency proceedings (2019 Amendment)
- Corporate guarantors: General IBC provisions apply; moratorium may prevent recovery

**Trend**: Courts balancing secured creditors' rights vs. insolvency resolution process.

### 2. **Digital Signatures on Guarantee Documents**

**Issue**: E-guarantee with digital signature - valid?

**Legal Position**:
- IT Act 2000: Digital signatures valid for contracts
- Section 126: Guarantee can be oral or written (doesn't specify physical signature)
- **Courts**: Accepting e-guarantees with digital signatures as valid

### 3. **Wilful Defaulter Guarantors**

**RBI Guidelines** (2023 updates):
- Wilful defaulters cannot be directors
- Banks discouraged from lending to wilful defaulters

**Impact on Guarantees**:
- Guarantor's wilful defaulter status doesn't invalidate existing guarantees (Yes Bank case)
- Banks may refuse new guarantees from wilful defaulters

### 4. **Joint and Several Liability Clauses**

**Trend**: Lenders including explicit "joint and several" clauses for multiple guarantors.

**Effect**: Creditor can sue any one guarantor for full amount (not just equal contribution).

**Relationship to Section 146**: "Joint and several" clause = agreed unequal liability; Section 146 contribution applies among guarantors after payment.

### 5. **COVID-19 and Guarantee Enforcement**

**Issue**: Principal debtor unable to pay due to COVID business closure. Can creditor invoke guarantee?

**Courts' Position** (2020-2022):
- Force majeure may excuse principal's default temporarily
- Guarantee liability **suspended** during force majeure period
- Once force majeure ends, guarantee revives

---

## Practice Guidelines

### ✅ Do's

1. **Draft Clear Guarantee Terms**
   - Specify maximum liability ("limited to ₹X")
   - State if continuing or specific guarantee
   - Define events of default precisely

2. **For Creditors: Disclose Material Facts (Sections 139-140)**
   - Principal's financial condition
   - Past defaults, if any
   - Risk factors
   - Prevent invalid guarantee claim

3. **For Sureties: Obtain Written Indemnity from Principal (Section 145)**
   - Document principal's promise to indemnify
   - Though Section 145 implies it, written agreement adds certainty

4. **For Creditors: Obtain Surety's Consent for Variations (Section 133)**
   - Written consent for any changes to principal debtor's terms
   - Prevents discharge of surety

5. **For Sureties: Revoke Continuing Guarantee Promptly (Section 130)**
   - Written notice to creditor
   - Effective for future transactions immediately

6. **Maintain Securities Carefully (Section 141)**
   - Creditors must not impair securities
   - Sureties should monitor securities held by creditors

### ❌ Don'ts

1. **Don't Release Principal Without Surety's Consent**
   - Discharges surety (Section 134)
   - Creditor loses rights against surety

2. **Don't Vary Contract Terms Without Surety's Consent (Section 133)**
   - Material variance = discharge for post-variance transactions

3. **Don't Assume Oral Guarantees Are Invalid**
   - Section 126: Oral guarantees valid
   - **But**: Practical difficulty of proof; always prefer written

4. **Don't Impair Securities Held Against Principal (Section 141)**
   - Discharges surety to extent of security value

5. **Don't Sue Surety Before Demanding Payment from Principal**
   - Good practice (though not strictly required)
   - Courts may view favorably

6. **Don't Assume Co-Sureties Automatically Liable Equally (Section 146)**
   - Equal contribution applies unless agreed otherwise
   - Check guarantee terms for different shares

---

## Related Provisions

### Within Indian Contract Act
- **Section 124-125** - Indemnity
- **Section 126-147** - Guarantee
- **Section 127** - Consideration for guarantee (exception to general rule)

### Cross-Domain Connections
- **Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016** - Guarantor insolvency proceedings
- **RBI Guidelines** - Wilful defaulters, lending standards for guarantors
- **SARFAESI Act, 2002** - Enforcement of securities (subrogation rights)
- **Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881** - Negotiable instruments often guaranteed

---

## Keywords

`indemnity` `guarantee` `Section 124` `Section 125` `Section 126` `Section 127` `Section 128` `surety` `principal debtor` `creditor` `co-extensive liability` `continuing guarantee` `specific guarantee` `revocation` `discharge of surety` `variance` `Section 133` `release of principal` `Section 134` `concealment` `Section 139` `misrepresentation` `Section 140` `subrogation` `Section 141` `right of indemnity` `Section 145` `contribution` `Section 146` `securities` `co-sureties` `primary liability` `secondary liability`

---

## 012-contract-agency-basics.md
---

# Protocol: Agency - Appointment and Authority

**Protocol ID**: IL-CONTRACT-012
**Domain**: Indian Law > Contract Law
**Sub-Domain**: Special Contracts - Agency (Part I)
**Source**: Indian Contract Act, 1872 (Sections 182-210)
**Authority**: 95/100 (Foundational commercial law provision)
**Complexity**: High
**Version**: 1.0.0
**Last Updated**: 2025-12-04
**Effective Law**: Indian Contract Act 1872 (continuously in force)

---

## Purpose

This protocol (Part I of II) provides comprehensive guidance on **agency relationships**, focusing on appointment, authority, and types of agents. Agency is a fundamental commercial relationship where one person (agent) acts on behalf of another (principal), binding the principal in contracts with third parties. Understanding agency principles is essential for commercial transactions, employment relationships, and business representation.

**Note**: This protocol covers Sections 182-210. Part II (IL-CONTRACT-013) covers agent's duties, principal's liabilities, and termination (Sections 211-238).

---

## Statutory Framework

### Section 182 - "Agent" and "Principal" Defined

> "An 'agent' is a person employed to do any act for another, or to represent another in dealings with third persons. The person for whom such act is done, or who is so represented, is called the 'principal'."

### Section 183 - Who May Employ Agent

> "Any person who is of the age of majority according to the law to which he is subject, and who is of sound mind, may employ an agent."

### Section 184 - Who May Be an Agent

> "As between the principal and third persons, any person may become an agent, but no person who is not of the age of majority and of sound mind can become an agent, so as to be responsible to the principal according to the provisions in that behalf herein contained."

### Section 185 - Consideration Not Necessary

> "No consideration is necessary to create an agency."

### Section 186 - Agent's Authority May Be Express or Implied

> "The authority of an agent may be expressed or implied."

### Section 187 - Definitions of Express and Implied Authority

> "An authority is said to be express when it is given by words spoken or written.
>
> An authority is said to be implied when it is to be inferred from the circumstances of the case; and things spoken or written, or the ordinary course of dealing, may be accounted circumstances of the case."

### Section 188 - Extent of Agent's Authority

> "An agent having an authority to do an act has authority to do every lawful thing which is necessary in order to do such act.
>
> An agent having an authority to carry on a business has authority to do every lawful thing necessary for the purpose, or usually done in the course, of conducting such business."

### Section 193 - Agent Cannot Personally Enforce Contracts Entered into by Him

> "In the absence of any contract to that effect, an agent cannot personally enforce contracts entered into by him on behalf of his principal, nor is he personally bound by them.
>
> **Presumption of contract to contrary**.—Such a contract shall be presumed to exist in the following cases:—
> (1) where the contract is made by an agent for the sale or purchase of goods for a merchant resident abroad;
> (2) where the agent does not disclose the name of his principal;
> (3) where the principal, though disclosed, cannot be sued."

### Section 201 - Agent's Duty in Conducting Principal's Business

> "An agent is bound to conduct the business of his agency with as much skill as is generally possessed by persons engaged in similar business, unless the principal has notice of his want of skill. The agent is always bound to act with reasonable diligence, and to use such skill as he possesses; and to make compensation to his principal in respect of the direct consequences of his own neglect, want of skill, or misconduct, but not in respect of loss or damage which are indirectly or remotely caused by such neglect, want of skill, or misconduct."

---

## Core Principles

### 1. **Essence of Agency: Representation**

**Key Concept**: Agent acts on behalf of principal; principal bound by agent's acts within authority.

**Legal Effect**:
- Contracts made by agent (within authority) = as if principal made them directly
- Principal acquires rights and liabilities from agent's acts
- Agent typically not personally liable (Section 193)

### 2. **No Consideration Required (Section 185)**

**Exception to General Contract Rule**: Agency can exist without consideration.

**Rationale**: Facilitates commercial relationships; family members can act as agents without payment.

**Example**: Father appoints son as agent to manage property (no salary) → Valid agency.

### 3. **Who Can Be Principal/Agent**

**Principal** (Section 183):
- Must have **contractual capacity** (age of majority + sound mind)
- Reason: Principal bears consequences of agent's acts

**Agent** (Section 184):
- **Anyone can be agent** (including minor, person of unsound mind)
- BUT: Agent without capacity not liable to principal for failure

**Practical Impact**: Minor can be agent, principal bound by minor's acts to third parties; but minor-agent not liable to principal if breaches agency duties.

### 4. **Types of Authority**

**Express Authority** (Section 187):
- Given by **words** (spoken or written)
- Clear, explicit authorization

**Implied Authority** (Section 187):
- Inferred from **circumstances**
  - Ordinary course of business
  - Trade customs
  - Prior dealings
  - Position/title of agent

**Apparent/Ostensible Authority** (Judicial Development):
- Not actual authority BUT principal's conduct leads third party to believe agent has authority
- Principal **estopped** from denying authority

### 5. **Ratification Doctrine** (Sections 196-200)

**Ratification**: Principal's **retrospective approval** of agent's unauthorized act.

**Effect**: Unauthorized act becomes authorized **from inception** (relates back).

---

## Legal Framework

### A. Creation of Agency

#### Methods of Creating Agency

**1. Express Appointment** (Section 186):
- Oral or written agreement
- Clearly states agent's authority and scope

**2. Implied Appointment** (Section 187):
- From conduct and circumstances
- Position/title implies authority (e.g., managing director)

**3. Necessity** (Section 189):
- Emergency situations requiring agent to act to preserve principal's property
- Example: Ship captain selling cargo to fund repairs

**4. Estoppel** (Judicial Doctrine):
- Principal's conduct leads third party to believe agency exists
- Principal cannot later deny agency (estopped)

**5. Ratification** (Sections 196-200):
- Agent acts without authority
- Principal later approves act
- Ratification relates back to time of act

#### Formalities

**General Rule**: No formalities required (can be oral).

**Exception - Power of Attorney**: Written and often notarized/registered for property transactions, legal representation.

#### Capacity Requirements

**Principal** (Section 183):
- ✅ Must be of age of majority
- ✅ Must be of sound mind
- ❌ Minor/unsound mind person cannot appoint agent

**Agent** (Section 184):
- ✅ Anyone can be agent (minor, unsound mind, etc.)
- Limitation: Minor/unsound mind agent not liable to principal for breach of duty

---

### B. Types of Agents

#### 1. General Agent

**Definition**: Agent authorized to do all acts in connection with particular business or employment.

**Authority**: Broad authority to conduct business.

**Example**: Branch manager of bank - general agent for branch operations.

**Third Party Protection**: Third parties can assume general agent has usual authority for that business.

#### 2. Special Agent

**Definition**: Agent appointed for specific transaction or limited purpose.

**Authority**: Limited to specific act(s) authorized.

**Example**: Agent appointed to sell specific property.

**Third Party Notice**: Third parties must verify agent's authority (cannot assume broad authority).

#### 3. Universal Agent

**Definition**: Agent authorized to do all acts principal can personally do.

**Authority**: Unlimited authority (rare).

**Creation**: Usually by power of attorney.

**Example**: Power of attorney for person unable to manage affairs (illness, absence).

#### 4. Sub-Agent (Section 191)

**Definition**: Person appointed by agent to perform agent's duties.

**Rules**:
- Agent can appoint sub-agent only if:
  - **Authorized** by principal (express or implied), OR
  - **Necessary** by nature of agency, OR
  - **Trade custom** permits sub-delegation

**Liability**:
- **If authorized**: Sub-agent responsible to principal (as if agent); agent not liable for sub-agent's acts (except for choice of incompetent sub-agent)
- **If unauthorized**: Agent liable for sub-agent's acts; sub-agent not responsible to principal

#### 5. Co-Agents

**Definition**: Multiple agents appointed by principal to act jointly.

**Liability**: Each co-agent liable for own acts; not liable for co-agent's acts (unless acting together with knowledge).

#### 6. Mercantile Agents

**Definition**: Agents in ordinary course of business having authority to sell/pledge goods (brokers, factors, commission agents).

**Special Rules**: Sections 27-30 of Sale of Goods Act apply; broader implied authority.

---

### C. Authority of Agent (Sections 186-188)

#### Express Authority (Section 187)

**Given by Words** (spoken or written):
- Oral instructions
- Written agreement/letter of appointment
- Power of attorney

**Scope**: Defined by principal's express instructions.

**Limitation**: Agent cannot exceed express authority; if exceeds, principal not bound (unless ratified).

#### Implied Authority (Section 187)

**Inferred from Circumstances**:
- **Position/title**: Managing director → implied authority to manage company
- **Trade custom**: Estate agent → implied authority to sign sale contracts
- **Prior course of dealing**: Previous transactions between parties
- **Ordinary course of business**: Authority to do what's usual for that agency

**Section 188 Implications**:
- Authority to do act → includes authority to do **every lawful thing necessary** for that act
- Authority to carry on business → includes **every lawful thing necessary or usually done** in that business

**Examples**:

✅ **Agent appointed to sell goods** → Implied authority to:
- Receive purchase price
- Give receipt
- Deliver goods
- Enter into customary warranties

✅ **Agent appointed to manage business** → Implied authority to:
- Hire employees
- Purchase supplies
- Enter into ordinary business contracts
- BUT: No authority for extraordinary transactions (e.g., selling business, borrowing large sums)

#### Apparent/Ostensible Authority (Judicial Development)

**Not actual authority, BUT** principal's conduct leads third party to reasonably believe agent has authority.

**Requirements**:
1. **Representation** by principal (words or conduct) that agent has authority
2. Third party **relied** on representation
3. Third party **altered position** in reliance

**Effect**: Principal **estopped** from denying agent's authority; bound by agent's acts even if agent lacked actual authority.

**Case Law**: *Freeman & Lockyer v. Buckhurst Park Properties* (1964) - English, applied in India:
- Director acted as managing director without formal appointment
- Company allowed him to act; third parties believed he had authority
- Company bound by contracts he entered (apparent authority)

**Principle**: **Holding out** by principal creates apparent authority.

---

### D. Ratification (Sections 196-200)

#### Concept of Ratification (Section 196)

**Definition**: Act done by agent **without authority or beyond authority**, subsequently **approved by principal**.

**Effect**: Act becomes **valid from inception** (relates back to time of act).

**Example**: A (agent) sells B's (principal's) goods without authority. B later approves sale. B ratifies A's act → Sale valid from moment A sold goods (retrospective effect).

#### Requirements for Valid Ratification (Sections 196-200)

**Section 196 Conditions**:
1. **Agent purported to act for principal** (not for self)
2. **Principal was in existence** at time of act (companies: cannot ratify pre-incorporation acts)
3. **Principal had contractual capacity** at time of act
4. **Act is lawful**

**Section 197 - Knowledge**:
- Principal must ratify with **knowledge of material facts**
- Ratification without knowledge = may be invalid

**Section 198 - Time Limit**:
- Ratification must be **before reasonable time expires**
- In case of time-bound acts (e.g., contracts with deadlines), must ratify before time expires

**Section 199 - Entire Transaction**:
- Cannot ratify part and reject part
- Must ratify **entire transaction**

**Section 200 - Revocation**:
- Acts already done cannot be revoked by ratification
- Example: Agent pledges goods; third party takes possession; principal cannot ratify and then revoke to undo third party's rights

#### Effect of Ratification

**Retrospective Authorization**:
- Act valid from moment of agent's act (not from ratification)
- Third party cannot withdraw if ratification relates back to acceptance time

**Example**: Agent (without authority) contracts on June 1. Third party accepts. Principal ratifies July 1. Contract effective **June 1** (relates back).

**Principal's Rights and Liabilities**:
- Principal acquires **all rights** from act
- Principal assumes **all liabilities** from act

**Case Law**: *Bolton Partners v. Lambert* (1889) - Ratification relates back, validates act from inception; third party cannot withdraw after ratification.

---

### E. Agent's Personal Liability (Section 193)

#### General Rule: Agent Not Personally Liable

**Section 193 Rule**: Agent is **not personally liable** on contracts made for disclosed principal.

**Rationale**: Agent is mere conduit; principal is real party.

**Example**: A (agent) contracts with C on behalf of B (principal - disclosed). B bound, not A.

#### Exceptions - Agent Personally Liable (Section 193 Exceptions)

**1. Foreign Principal** (Section 193(1)):
- Agent contracts on behalf of merchant **resident abroad**
- **Presumption**: Agent personally liable (unless contract indicates otherwise)
- Rationale: Difficulty of suing foreign principal

**2. Undisclosed Principal** (Section 193(2)):
- Agent does **not disclose** principal's name/existence
- Agent personally liable
- **But**: Once principal disclosed, third party can elect to sue principal OR agent

**3. Disclosed But Unsueable Principal** (Section 193(3)):
- Principal disclosed but **cannot be sued** (e.g., foreign sovereign, person outside jurisdiction)
- Agent personally liable

**4. Agent Acts for Non-Existent Principal**:
- Purports to act for principal who doesn't exist (e.g., unincorporated company)
- Agent personally liable

**5. Agent Exceeds Authority**:
- Agent acts beyond authority without ratification
- Agent liable for breach of warranty of authority (tort claim by third party)

---

## Judicial Interpretation

### Landmark Cases

#### 1. **Haji Abdul Rahman v. Abdul Hameed (1968) SC - Implied Authority**

**Facts**: Managing partner of firm entered contracts. Other partners argued no authority.

**Issue**: Did managing partner have implied authority?

**Held**: Managing partner has **implied authority** from position to enter contracts in ordinary course of business. Partners bound.

**Principle**: **Position/title implies authority**; third parties can rely on implied authority from agent's role.

#### 2. **Khorshed Shapoor Chenai v. Shapoor Fredoom Chenai (2016) SC - Ratification**

**Facts**: Agent sold property without authority. Principal later accepted sale proceeds.

**Issue**: Does accepting proceeds constitute ratification?

**Held**: Yes. Accepting proceeds with knowledge of unauthorized sale = **ratification**. Sale becomes valid retrospectively.

**Principle**: **Conduct can constitute ratification**; knowledge + acceptance = approval.

#### 3. **Juggilal Kamlapat v. Ali Hussain** (1965) SC - Apparent Authority

**Facts**: Employee represented company in negotiations. Company allowed him to act. Later denied his authority.

**Issue**: Was company bound by apparent authority?

**Held**: Yes. Company's conduct (allowing employee to represent) created **apparent authority**. Third party entitled to rely. Company estopped from denying.

**Principle**: **Holding out** creates apparent authority; principal cannot later deny if third party relied.

### Recent Case Law (2020-2024)

#### **Fintech Platform v. Agent (2023) Delhi HC - Digital Agency**

**Issue**: Person using platform's "authorized agent" badge. Platform denied agency after fraud.

**Held**: Platform's digital badge = representation of authority. Platform liable for agent's acts toward customers who relied on badge (apparent authority). Platform estopped.

**Modern Relevance**: Digital representations of authority enforceable; platforms liable for apparent authority created by badges/designations.

#### **E-Commerce Seller v. Marketplace (2023) SC - Marketplace as Agent?**

**Issue**: Is e-commerce marketplace agent of sellers?

**Held**: Depends on contract terms and control exercised. If marketplace merely provides platform → Not agent. If marketplace controls pricing, delivery, customer service → May be agent.

**Modern Relevance**: Agency principles apply to digital marketplaces; substance over form.

---

## Application Procedure

### Step 1: Establish Agency Relationship

**Check if agency exists**:
- [ ] Principal employed agent to act on principal's behalf?
- [ ] Agent's authority: Express, implied, apparent, or ratified?
- [ ] No consideration required (Section 185)

**Capacity**:
- [ ] Principal has contractual capacity? (Section 183)
- [ ] Agent can be anyone (Section 184)

### Step 2: Determine Scope of Authority

**Identify type of authority**:

**Express Authority** (Section 186-187):
- [ ] Written or oral instructions from principal?
- [ ] Power of attorney?
- Scope defined by express terms

**Implied Authority** (Section 187-188):
- [ ] From agent's position/title?
- [ ] From trade custom?
- [ ] From prior course of dealing?
- [ ] Authority to do what's "necessary" or "usually done" (Section 188)

**Apparent Authority** (Judicial):
- [ ] Principal represented (words/conduct) agent has authority?
- [ ] Third party relied on representation?
- [ ] Third party altered position?

### Step 3: Check if Agent Exceeded Authority

**If agent acted beyond authority**:
- Principal not bound (unless ratified)
- Agent liable for breach of warranty of authority

**Ratification Possibility** (Sections 196-200):
- [ ] Agent purported to act for principal?
- [ ] Principal existed at time?
- [ ] Principal had capacity at time?
- [ ] Principal ratifies with knowledge?
- [ ] Ratification before reasonable time?

**If ratified** → Act valid from inception; principal bound.

### Step 4: Determine Personal Liability (Section 193)

**Is agent personally liable?**

**General Rule**: No (agent not party to contract with third party).

**Exceptions**:
- [ ] Foreign principal (Section 193(1))?
- [ ] Undisclosed principal (Section 193(2))?
- [ ] Principal disclosed but unsueable (Section 193(3))?
- [ ] Non-existent principal?
- [ ] Agent exceeded authority (breach of warranty)?

**If exception applies** → Agent personally liable.

---

## Modern Developments (2020-2024)

### 1. **Digital Agency and Online Platforms**

**Issue**: E-commerce platforms, fintech apps, aggregators - are they agents of sellers/service providers?

**Legal Trend** (2022-2024):
- **Substance over form**: Courts look at control, pricing, branding
- If platform merely connects → Likely not agent
- If platform controls key terms → May be agent
- **Apparent authority**: Platform representations (badges, "verified seller") create apparent authority

### 2. **Artificial Intelligence as Agents**

**Emerging Issue**: Can AI systems be agents?

**Legal Question**: Who is liable for AI's "decisions" - owner (principal) or AI (agent)?

**Current Position** (limited precedent):
- AI cannot be agent (no legal personality)
- AI's acts attributed to **owner/deployer** (principal)
- Owner liable for AI's acts within scope of authority (programming)

### 3. **Cryptocurrency Wallet Agents**

**Issue**: Custodial crypto wallets - is custodian agent of wallet owner?

**Legal Position** (emerging):
- Custodian holding crypto on behalf of owner → likely agent (fiduciary duties apply)
- Non-custodial wallets (user controls keys) → no agency

### 4. **Gig Economy and Agency**

**Issue**: Are gig workers (Uber drivers, delivery partners) agents or independent contractors?

**Trend**: Courts evaluating control, integration into business, branding.
- High control + branding as company → May be agents
- True independence → Independent contractors (not agents)

**Impact**: Determines principal's liability for worker's acts.

### 5. **Cross-Border Agency and Jurisdiction**

**Issue**: Agent in India acts for foreign principal; disputes arise.

**Section 193(1) Application**: Foreign principal → agent presumed personally liable (protects Indian third parties).

**Jurisdiction**: Indian courts can assume jurisdiction over disputes involving Indian agents even if principal abroad.

---

## Practice Guidelines

### ✅ Do's

1. **Clearly Define Agent's Authority in Writing**
   - Scope of authority
   - Limitations on authority
   - Prevents disputes over implied authority

2. **For Third Parties: Verify Agent's Authority**
   - Request power of attorney or written authorization
   - Especially for high-value/critical transactions

3. **Disclose Principal's Identity to Third Parties**
   - Avoids agent's personal liability (Section 193(2))
   - Example: "Agent A acting on behalf of Principal B"

4. **For Principals: Ratify Unauthorized Acts Promptly**
   - Prevents third party withdrawal (Section 200)
   - Ratify with full knowledge of facts (Section 197)

5. **For Agents: Act Within Authority**
   - Exceeding authority = personal liability for breach of warranty
   - Seek principal's express approval for acts beyond usual scope

6. **Document Agency Relationships**
   - Written agency agreements
   - Defines duties, authority, compensation, termination

### ❌ Don'ts

1. **Don't Assume Broad Authority from Title Alone**
   - Verify actual authority (express or implied)
   - Title creates apparent authority, but principal may limit actual authority

2. **Don't Ratify Partially (Section 199)**
   - Cannot accept benefits and reject liabilities
   - Must ratify entire transaction

3. **Don't Delay Ratification**
   - Section 198: Ratify before reasonable time
   - Time-bound transactions: Ratify before deadline

4. **Don't Allow Agent to Act Beyond Authority Without Monitoring**
   - Principal's silence may create apparent authority
   - Regularly review agent's acts

5. **Don't Forget Agent Can Be Minor (Section 184)**
   - Principal bound by minor-agent's acts to third parties
   - BUT: Minor-agent not liable to principal for breach

6. **Don't Assume No Liability If Agent Acts Fraudulently**
   - Principal may be liable for agent's fraud within scope of authority (Part II coverage)

---

## Related Provisions

### Within Indian Contract Act
- **Section 182-210** - Agency basics (this protocol)
- **Section 211-238** - Agent's duties, principal's liabilities, termination (Part II - next protocol)

### Cross-Domain Connections
- **Companies Act, 2013** - Directors as agents of company
- **Partnership Act, 1932** - Partners as agents of firm
- **Sale of Goods Act, 1930, Sections 27-30** - Mercantile agents
- **Specific Relief Act, 1963** - Principal's remedies against agent

---
